#166. The $500 billion beauty industry's 'green' ambitions are a patchwork at best. And they're falling short

The escalating climate crisis is shifting many people’s purchasing patterns and this extends to the $500 billion dollar global beauty industry which is grappling with a range of sustainability challenges across product manufacturing, packaging and disposal.

'Delay means death': We're running out of ways to adapt to the climate crisis, new report shows. Here are the key takeaways

Strategy and consulting firm Simon Kucher’s Global Sustainability Study 2021 found 60% of consumers around the world rated sustainability as an important purchase criterion, and 35% were willing to pay more for sustainable products or services.

This shift in consumer preferences has propelled many beauty brands to set environmental goals: to move away from single-use and virgin plastics, provide recyclable, reusable and refillable packaging and offer more transparency around products’ ingredients so customers can ascertain how “green” their purchase is.

However, consumers still struggle to understand the sustainability credentials of many products, according to the British Beauty Council. This is because the industry’s clean-up efforts have been inconsistent, and fall short of making a recognizable impact in the absence of collective goal-setting, global strategy and standardized regulations.

There is no international standard for the beauty industry on how much product ingredient information to share with customers — or how to do so. Brands can set their own rules and goals, giving rise to confusion and “greenwashing,” where sustainability claims are often touted but not substantiated.

Companies often use marketing language like “clean beauty” to make it seem like their products are natural, for example, when they may not actually be organic, sustainable or ethically made.

After rebranding in the West, many beauty companies are still offering to 'whiten' skin elsewhere

“The term ‘clean beauty’ has become quite dangerous. It’s used to sell more products,” according to British Beauty Council CEO Millie Kendall, who added that such buzzwords are losing traction in the UK as British customers wise up to their shortcomings. “Customers need better marketing information and certification information.”

In a 2021 report calling on the industry to have “the courage to change” their business practices, the British Beauty Council wrote that, all too often, even natural ingredients involved in manufacturing products give way to “over-consumption, non-regenerative farming practices, pollution, waste and neglect.”

“The only way out of this is transparency,” Kendall told CNN.

Jen Lee, chief impact officer at US-based brand Beautycounter, said she continues to see confusion over ingredients among consumers. (In 2013, the company launched and published “The Never List,” which currently cites more than 2,800 chemicals — including heavy metals, parabens and formaldehyde — it claims to never use in its products.)

“Natural vs. synthetic ingredients has been a conversation. People think natural is safer, but it’s not always the case,” Lee explained. “Natural ingredients formulated in the industry can have toxic load. Heavy metals can occur in natural components of the earth.”

Mother loses peripheral vision from apparent exposure to mercury in beauty creams. Toxic levels in her home put family at risk, say experts

“We used to be more natural and organic,” added Sasha Plavsic, founder of makeup brand ILIA Beauty. “What was challenging is (that) raw materials were difficult to source or would come in inconsistently or products wouldn’t perform.”

Most makeup is created and molded at high temperatures, Plavsic explained. Purely organic materials often fall apart in this heat, leading to inconsistent results and subpar product performance. “Not every synthetic is bad,” Plavsic said. “Sometimes, it helps create the best in class formula.”

The industry’s plastic packaging is a particular sustainability challenge — 95% is thrown away and the vast majority is not recycled, according to the British Beauty Council.

The cosmetics business is the fourth biggest plastic packaging user globally — after food and beverage, industrial packaging and pharmaceuticals — and plastic is about 67% of the industry’s packaging volume, according to Vantage Market Research. Beauty giant L’Oreal used 144,430 metric tons of plastic in its packaging material in 2021, for example, according to the Ellen Macarthur Foundation (EMF). Estee Lauder Companies reported its brands produced 71,600 metric tons of plastic in product packaging that same year.

And only 9% of the global plastic waste is recycled, according to a report from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development. The United States only recycles 4% of its plastic waste.

Single-use plastic is wreaking havoc on the planet. Here's what you can do to minimize your impact

Many brands are trying to phase out harmful plastics from their operations and adopt post-consumer recycled (PCR) plastic. (L’Oreal has set a target of 50% PCR plastic usage by 2025, while Estee Lauder is targeting 25% “or more” PCR plastic — but both are far from achieving their targets.)

“Between 60-70 major global brands have made unprecedented progress” in PCR plastic usage across industries, EMF’s Plastic Initiative Lead Sander DeFruyt told CNN. But DeFruyt stressed that PCR plastic must be adopted in conjunction with brands removing single and virgin plastics from their usage cycles to truly make a difference.

However, PCR plastic is not easy to find — low recycling rates around the world mean there is limited supply. Meanwhile, demand for it is growing demand across industries, DeFruyt said. This competition hikes up its price, which is already higher than virgin plastic.

Hair care brand FEKKAI claims that it used up to 95% PCR content in its packaging, but pricing and supply issues posed a challenge, forcing it to currently aim for containers and packaging that feature at least 50% PCR in its packaging.

“PCR plastic is more expensive than stock plastic. The cost is hard and then sourcing it is too,” founder Frédéric Fekkai told CNN. “PCR is close to our heart, but there is a massive demand, so finding recycled plastic is difficult.”

Beauty retailers plays a pivotal — and under-utilized — role, with control over stocking decisions and supply chains. But many vary when it comes to the standards they set for brands they sell.

“Smaller businesses do more, full stop,” said Jessi Baker, founder of the technology platform Provenance, which helps brands display their sustainability credentials for customers. “They move more nimbly. Some of them are born-good brands — climate friendliness was part of their setup. They don’t need to restructure their entire supply chain. Their culture already has it compared to the larger brands who need to work hard to change.”

A history of beauty trends -- and the standards that shaped them

Sephora launched its “Clean + Planet Positive” initiative in 2021, which labeled products that met its set criteria. (This is separate from the French retailer’s “Clean at Sephora” program, which is currently facing a consumer lawsuit alleging it carries a significant percentage of products understood by customers to be harmful.) Target launched a similar program in 2022, featuring a “Target Zero” icon for both online and in-store offerings that either have reusable, recyclable, compostable or reduced plastic packaging, or feature waterless or concentrated products.

Still, many steps taken by brands and retailers do not even begin to touch on the waste and pollution generated throughout supply chains, manufacturing and shipping, all huge problems for the industry to grapple with.

The gaps in standardization in the beauty ecosystem can, to some extent, be filled by certifications such as the US-born B Corporation, or B Corp. This accreditation, one of the most well-known in the beauty space, is issued by the non-profit B Lab, which scores a company on a variety of criteria around ethics and sustainability. However beneficial it may be among eco-conscious consumers, though, it is currently completely voluntary for brands to apply for.

Governments and multinationals enforcing regulations and setting a base line for brands to operate from when making sustainability claims would go a long way to making change, many experts and business leaders believe.

Susanne Kaufmann, founder of her namesake beauty brand, says her efforts in Austria would reap better results if more countries around the world had stricter, more uniform garbage disposal laws.

A trash heap 62 meters high shows the scale of India's climate challenge

“I package our product in a recyclable material,” Kaufmann said. (Her products’ packaging, which is refillable and reusable, is made from 75% recycled plastic — and is 100% recyclable.) If I send this to the US, the garbage is not separated… and it’s not recyclable,” she explained, referring to inconsistencies in recycling laws across the United States.

And when it comes to ingredients, the European Chemicals Agency lists 2,495 substances banned from use in cosmetic products marketed for sale or use in the bloc. But the US Food and Drug administration only lists 11, making it more challenging for American consumers to find safer, greener options. The Environmental Working Group, a non-profit watchdog, studied lab tests of 51 sunscreen products in 2021 and found that only 35% of products met the EU standard, compared with 94% that passed the US standard.

However, while government can set minimum requirements, Mia Davis, vice president of sustainability and impact at beauty retailer Credo Beauty, says the needle will move in the private sector.

Sustainable ships: The world's most eco-conscious cruises

“Regulation can raise the floor a bit. A person who doesn’t know about any (sustainability issues) should still be able to walk into a bodega and get clean products… But that’s never going to be what the market can do,” she said. “Market leadership is key.”

In the absence of bold regulations or global standards on sustainability practices, this “leadership” — undertaken both by brands and customers in the beauty marketplace — is likely to be the most immediately impactful vector for addressing the industry’s climate shortcomings. It will take continued collective advocacy and initiative to see meaningful climate-conscious change.

#165. Does the Oleshky ceasefire signal progress to come in Ukraine?

Hundreds of civilians were evacuated from the city of Oleshky after Russia and Ukraine agreed to a US-brokered ceasefire. Some observers have expressed optimism that more such humanitarian missions might follow.

Nearly 800 residents have been evacuated from the Russia-occupied city of Oleshky in southern Ukraine. This was announced by US first lady Melania Trump, who was credited with helping to mediate the ceasefire. The mission was coordinated by Ukrainian Human Rights Commissioner Dmytro Lubinets and his Russian counterpart, Yana Lantratova.

Most evacuees chose to be sent to Ukrainian-controlled territory, although some opted for transfer to Russia. Currently, more than 1,000 people remain in Oleshky, according to Kherson's regional administration. They were supplied with food, water and medicine.

Melania Trump hailed the mission as a success, taking to social media to thank Kyiv and Moscow for their "leadership and cooperation." She said the Oleshky ceasefire could go on to inspire a "lasting peace between the two peoples."

Dmytro Lubinez similarly wrote on the Telegram messaging platform that it is "important to maintain this cooperation mechanism," adding that "we are currently exploring options with Yana Lantratova to continue similar humanitarian efforts."

The Kremlin, however, was quick to dampen any such expectations, making clear that no further plans for humanitarian missions exist at this stage.

One day after the ceasefire ended, Ukraine's general staff stated that Russia had "used the humanitarian pause to fortify its positions." It also said Russia had carried out troop rotations, expanded its anti-drone defenses, and brought in ammunition.

British broadcaster Sky News aired drone footage, produced with the support of the Ukrainian army, showing the Russian army transporting heavy equipment, weapons, personnel and food to Oleshky and neighboring villages.

"It was clear to the Ukrainian government and international mediators that Russia could exploit the pause in this way. But it was the right thing to do to allow people to leave the country," Volodymyr Vyatrovych of the Ukrainian European Solidarity opposition party wrote on Facebook.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

He believes Russia would do the exact same thing if Kyiv were to withdraw its troops from the Donetsk region, as Russian President Vladimir Putin is demanding.

"Russia would use this to prepare for its next offensive, to further destroy Ukraine," Vyatrovych wrote. That said, humanitarian missions have their place, says Ihor Chalenko of the Ukrainian Center for Analysis and Strategies. "We shouldn't speak ill of the Oleshky mission, even though it's clear that the Russians have used the ceasefire in the occupied territories to their advantage," Chalenko told DW. That's why he says Kyiv should insist on banning such behavior in future ceasefires.

The Oleshky mission took place just shortly before US voters headed to the polls in the midterm elections. Dmytro Levus of the United Ukraine research center believes the timing was no coincidence. "The Russian side played right into the hands of US President Donald Trump, whose Republican Party was expected to face a crushing electoral defeat," Levus said.

Involving Melania in the mediation was a "subtle public relations move" for Trump, through which he hoped to score points ahead of the election, Levus explained. First Lady Melania Trump has long been an advocate for humanitarian causes.

Chalenko says the Oleshky ceasefire was in Washington's interest because it allowed Donald Trump to cast himself as a "peacemaker."

Moscow also hoped it could improve its image through the humanitarian mission, especially as it caused Oleshky's humanitarian catastrophe in the first place, Chalenko said. Rescuing civilians is intended to serve as proof that "not everything is bad in Russia."

Cooperation between the two human rights ombudspersons had reached "a new level," Chalenko said, adding that the Oleshky case was just one of several noteworthy examples that also included prisoner swaps and foreign representatives gaining access to Ukrainian political prisoners held in Russia.

He does not, however, believe that Russian Human Rights Commissioner Yana Lantratova makes her own decisions. "We are seeing a certain top-down realignment of Russian policy," Chalenko told DW. He also said it was important to further develop this communication channel. The lessons learned from the evacuation of Oleshky could be applied to other towns along the front, Chalenko believes.

Dmytro Levus says the Oleshky mission has proven that, if Moscow were so inclined, a ceasefire could be established along the entire front line. Yet Russia has so far shown no willingness in doing so, partly because Ukraine has not agreed to Putin's terms, Levus explains.

Preparations are underway for a new round of negotiations between Ukraine, the US and Russia on November 10 and 11 in Abu Dhabi. Dmytro Levus believes the Oleshky ceasefire could provide substance to talks on humanitarian issues. He does not, however, think there will be much progress on a political level, even though he does see scope for a ceasefire at sea, for example.

Levus says that the US will be particularly keen to present further humanitarian successes. Ukraine may use the talks to negotiate a new prisoner exchange, though Levus says Russia will likely only budge under pressure.

Ihor Chalenko says one should not expect too much of the upcoming talks. He believes that Russia is looking for a pretext to withdraw from the negotiations, preferring instead to expand its cooperation with the US, without wanting to make any concessions to end the Ukraine war.

Samuel Greene of King's College London is similarly skeptical. "I assume that at some point, the Kremlin will come to realize that there is nothing left to gain on the battlefield and that they will have to reach an agreement."

"That moment hasn't yet come though," Greene told DW, adding that Russia was still trying to buy time by feigning a willingness to engage in dialogue with the US.

#164. Police investigating two further rape allegations against Russell Brand

Detective urges other potential victims to come forward and says: ‘Our work’s not done’

The lead detective in the case against Russell Brand has urged other potential victims of the comedian to come forward after it emerged he is under investigation for two further rape allegations and faces a civil claim in the UK.

Det Supt Andy Furphy, the Metropolitan police’s strategic lead in the investigation, described Brand, 51, as “a predator” whose behaviour escalated as his fame grew.

On Thursday, Brand was found guilty of raping a fan and sexually assaulting three women over five years at the height of his career. He was cleared of two further counts of rape and one count of attempted indecent assault after a four-week trial at Southwark crown court.

It can now be revealed that Brand is under investigation for two further rape allegations and that the Met has submitted files to the Crown Prosecution Service.

Brand has also been accused of sexually assaulting a woman at his mansion in Los Angeles in 2008, in a civil claim filed at the high court in London.

The Met’s investigation into Brand, named Operation Actinolite, involved tracing and speaking to more than 100 witnesses, reviewing more than 1,400 documents and taking more than 340 statements, including from Brand’s former employers, colleagues and associates.

Speaking after Brand’s conviction, Furphy said the police investigation was continuing and he believed there could be more victims.

“If you get a conviction then it tends to encourage other people to come forward because they see the courage and the bravery and the resilience that these women have had to go through this criminal justice process, and they feel their own strength as a result of that. They want to come forward and have their story told,” he told the Guardian.

“So, yes, there may be more victims of Russell Brand out there, but for now our focus remains on the women that came forward.”

The Met said Brand had been interviewed under caution in connection with two further rape allegations made by two women. “Files have been submitted to the Crown Prosecution Service in relation to both complainants. Detectives remain in close contact with prosecutors as inquiries continue and further investigative work is carried out,” a spokesperson said.

Pre-trial legal documents suggested there were outstanding charges against Brand in relation to a woman who made allegations about him in a Channel 4 Dispatches documentary in 2023. She accused Brand of grooming her and sending a BBC car to bring her to him from the gates of her school when she was a 16-year-old pupil.

Furphy refused to comment on any outstanding inquiries but appealed for women to come forward if they had anything to report.

“We have a live investigation. Our work’s not done,” Furphy said. “What I would say, is if you’ve been affected by the case, if Russell Brand has committed a crime against you, rape or serious sexual violence or a sex assault, whatever it might be, and you haven’t spoken to police yet, then come forward. We’ve got specially trained staff, specially trained officers. We’ll support you and listen to you.”

It is understood a couple of other women made allegations against Brand during the Met’s investigation but they did not result in charges.

Furphy said Brand had abused his celebrity status to assault women who then feared they would not be believed.

“Russell Brand is a predator,” Furphy said. “He preys on women and commits serious sexual offences against them. He’s in a position of power, a celebrity, he’s wealthy.

“If you look at the offending from the first date up until when the final offences were committed, you can see an escalation in his behaviour. The more famous he gets, the more emboldened he gets, less likely to be challenged, less likely to be held accountable. He’s been held accountable now.”

He praised the “really courageous, brave and resilient women” who gave evidence against him while facing the intense scrutiny of Brand’s barrister, Ian Winter KC. He described the cross-examination as “deeply uncomfortable”.

“Rape and serious sexual offences is a deeply personal thing, people are recounting some of the worst things that have ever happened to them,” he said. “We can perceive that line of questioning may come, but I’ve got to be honest with you, it was deeply uncomfortable at times.”

Brand gave interviews to Tucker Carlson, Megyn Kelly and Piers Morgan before the trial and continued podcasting throughout, which the detective said had taken a toll on the women.

“It’d be fair to say that what he did to them has had a very long-lasting effect on them, in the way they trust people … it’s been devastating to many of them,” Furphy said.

“Then a challenging aspect of this [case] was the constant public commentary around it, and some of that was coming from him. And as you can imagine in a case like this, as an investigation team, we’ve got to review everything – DVDs, books, podcasts, stuff that’s appearing online.

“So, for me, he drove right up to the line of what was acceptable in a live criminal trial and stopped just short. But that doesn’t mean we weren’t prepared to take action if it started to cross over that line, and the women who’ve put their trust in us knew that.”

Furphy said the case may be a “watershed moment” in inspiring a wave of other victims of sexual offences to come forward. “I think if you look back to that culture during that period of time, that kind of lad culture, there may be other victims that come forward regarding other people.”

Brand is already facing civil claims in Britain and the US. In court papers seen by the Guardian, a woman who has begun civil proceedings in London has claimed Brand forcibly removed her dress, shoes and underwear, pushed her on to his bed and attempted “to perform oral sex on her against her will”.

She said she eventually managed to break free from his grasp but was left “terrified and shaking violently” as she ran from the house. She said she later suffered from PTSD symptoms. Brand denies her claims, which he described via his lawyers as “fundamentally dishonest”.

The case was lodged last year but was stayed until the proceedings in the criminal case against Brand concluded this week. A claim form filed in February 2025 suggests the woman is seeking damages of up to £100,000.

The woman said she felt “traumatised by the rage Brand displayed when she refused to submit to him sexually” and feared her safety would be at risk if she pursued a criminal complaint.

However, she decided to report her allegation to the Met in 2025. She said she was “fortified” by other women who had come forward to make allegations against Brand.

The woman’s case was not included in the trial. The Guardian understands this was largely because the offence was alleged to have occurred in the US, meaning the English criminal courts did not have jurisdiction to prosecute.

A separate civil claim was filed in the US in 2023 over allegations Brand sexually assaulted a woman in 2010 while filming the movie Arthur.

None of the victims in the criminal case have submitted compensation claims.

#159. Pete Hegseth under fire as Republicans trade blame for midterm debacle

Iran war hawk Pete Hegseth is facing the fallout from Republicans' midterms losses as high energy prices turned voters toward Democrats. US President Trump continues to support his embattled defense chief — for now.

Republicans in Washington continued to assess the damage on Thursday, after a resounding electoral defeat in the midterm elections two days prior saw Democrats take over Congress by large margins.

Republican Party insiders told US outlets CNN and The Washington Post that Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth is feeling the heat over criticism that the war in Iran, which he supports, cost his party the elections.

Trump on Wednesday acknowledged that the Iran war affected the midterms, saying that voters were frustrated how the 8-month-long conflict has cause gas and diesel prices to skyrocket.

"The Iran thing we had to do, and we continue to do it," Trump told reporters, but he ultimately defended the war effort and denied any plans to fire his defense secretary over it, saying Hegseth was "doing a very good job."

But US Vice President JD Vance has sought to distance himself from the war, questioning whether it was worth the cost.

"The ⁠reason ⁠we lost is the war," Vance said on X. "If you'd like to argue it was worth it, that losing the Senate for the next 6 years was worth it, then do so."

Hegseth has been a loud voice in Washington for the US attacking Iran, and has publicly chastised lawmakers and journalists for raising questions about the conflict's strategic objectives, cost and lack of congressional authorization.

The defense secretary has also been accused of not being forthright about the damage to US bases in the Middle East and the severity of troop casualties. He has also purged dozens of high ranking military officers, while denying promotions to others on dubious grounds.

Hegseth was in hot water even before the Iran war. In October 2025, a Signal group chat between himself and Vice President JD Vance discussing a military strike on Yemen in October 2025 accidentally included a journalist, and led to calls for Hegseth's dismissal.

According to CNN's reporting, multiple Republicans told the network on condition of anonymity that many in their party are now turning against the defense secretary.

Sources also told the Washington Post that the political situation at the Defense Department appears "very dynamic at the moment," with "knives out."

With Trump backing Hegseth for now, The Post reported that those close to the Trump administration have floated the notion that the president might dismiss General Dan Caine, the chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, or Admiral Brad Cooper, who has overseen the war, instead of the embattled Hegseth.

Pressure is mounting in Capitol Hill, as Hegseth's critics from both sides of the aisle turn their focus on him.

CNN reported that House Republicans are expected to hold an impeachment vote to directly repudiate the defense secretary in a matter of days.

Republican Representative Thomas Massie of Kentucky, who has been a critic of Trump, told CNN he plans to trigger the impeachment, which multiple GOP sources say is expect to pass.

In September, Massie had said he would force a vote in the House on impeaching Hegseth, which led to House Speaker Mike Johnson sending Congress into recess until after the midterms.

On Thursday, Massie posted on X that his impeachment resolution is a "much gentler" version of what's coming when the Democrats take over Congress in January.

"They'll have hearings & trials," he wrote.

Chief Pentagon spokesman Sean Parnell said that Hegseth "will not back down or be deterred by politicians seeking to score partisan points or launch phony investigations where no wrongdoing exists."

"We will continue to drive President Trump's agenda at the Department of War — despite threats of sham witch hunts from Democrats — and Secretary Hegseth is 100% committed to leading the Department until the end of President Trump's term in office," Parnell added, using the administration's name for the Department of Defense.

Fresh off their sweeping wins, Democrats will have the power to launch investigations on Hegseth and even slash funding for the Defense Department and the Iran war.

"I'm going after Pete Hegseth. Not only is he incompetent and corrupt, he's undermining our national security and the safety of our troops," said Representative Jason Crow, a Democratic member of the House Armed Services Committee.

Representative Adam Smith, the top Democrat on the committee, said a top priority would be "to conduct vigorous oversight of the Department of Defense and the Trump Administration," including on "the illegal war against Iran."

#158. British Ebola patient evacuated from DR Congo arrives at London hospital

Doctor who was helping response to outbreak in Africa taken to specialist isolation unit at Royal Free hospital

A British doctor who tested positive for Ebola while working in the Democratic Republic of the Congo has returned to the UK for specialist treatment.

The patient, whose identity is unknown, was transported home on a medical evacuation flight, the Department of Health and Social Care said on Thursday. They are now being cared for in a specialised high-level isolation unit at the Royal Free hospital in Hampstead, north London.

Dr Renu Bindra, the deputy director for all-hazards public health response at the UK Health Security Agency, said: “UKHSA and the NHS have well-established and robust infection prevention and control protocols to limit the risk of onward transmission, and the risk to the wider public remains low.”

A humanitarian organisation sent the doctor to DR Congo to help in the response to the outbreak, which is the second largest on record. It is the first case involving a British national contracting Ebola in the current outbreak.

The Royal Free's isolation unit has strict protocols in place to prevent the patient coming into contact with any members of the public, and all staff treating the patient are equipped with the necessary personal protective equipment alongside other measures to prevent transmission.

Ebola is a highly contagious, severe and often fatal illness spread through direct contact with body fluids such as the blood or vomit of infected people, or dead bodies.

The current outbreak, declared in May by DR Congo’s health ministry, has surpassed 10,000 cases and 5,000 deaths. It has spread to Kenya and Uganda in east Africa, and last month Nigeria confirmed a case in its capital, Abuja.

People with the disease tend to fall ill two to 21 days after contact with an orthoebolavirus. Initial symptoms include fever, aches, pains, and fatigue, but can then progress to diarrhoea, vomiting and unexplained bleeding.

There have only been four confirmed cases of the virus in the UK previously. A case in 1976 was from a laboratory needle incident, and the three others involved health workers who were responding to an outbreak in West Africa between 2014 and 2015.

#155. Americans voted against Trump's Iran war. Gulf countries hope that means an end to the conflict

The US midterm results showed how unpopular Donald Trump's Iran war is. Now, Gulf countries, trapped for months in the middle of the war between the US and Iran, see reason for optimism.

After his party's resounding defeat in the US midterm elections this week, President Donald Trump conceded that the war with Iran had likely played a part.

"I think a part of it was Iran, but, at the same time, people understood the importance of what I've done with Iran," Trump told Fox News, conceding that US voters were unwilling to accept the impact of the war on gas prices.

Observers say the United States and Israel had been holding off on major attacks on Iran until after both had held elections.

At the end of October, the Wall Street Journal reported that the Trump administration was already warning Gulf state governments to brace for a short, intense military campaign after the midterms.

While foreign policy is a US president's purview, analysts have pointed out that a Congress controlled by the Democratic party could ostensibly withhold funding for Trump's military expeditions. However newly-elected Democratic politicians won't take their seats until early next year.

As two US professors of political science wrote for Foreign Affairs magazine this week, past American presidents, like Trump, who become "lame ducks" — that is, a president who loses control of Congress and can't run for another term — have often ramped up international interventions.

"As they think about their legacy, lame ducks often turn to foreign policy, where the power of the presidency allows them to act unfettered by pesky domestic politics," they explained. And one of the places where Trump is most likely to act is Iran, the professors argued.

On the other hand, Trump also told several media outlets that he was in "no rush" to start bombing Iran again.

Opinions on Iran's next moves are also divided.

The Iranians have said they're ready for any attack and rhetoric from senior leaders indicates they may be willing to strike a wider range of targets than previously. That could include regional energy and economic infrastructure and even undersea telecommunications cabling or data centers.

If Iran thinks there is an existential threat to its leadership, then "all bets are off," Ross Harrison, a senior fellow at the Washington-based Middle East Institute, or MEI, and an expert on Iran, said during a virtual briefing hosted by MEI this week. "I think they will target differently in the Gulf states [this time] … so it really depends on how all this unfolds."

But other experts believe Iran was deliberately holding out to pressure Trump before the midterms. Now the election is over, the Iranians may be more willing to negotiate a peace deal.

All this leaves the Gulf states, who have been the main targets of Iranian attacks since the conflict began, in a difficult position.

"We find ourselves drawn within a crisis that we worked diplomatically very hard to try to avert," Anwar Gargash, a foreign policy adviser to United Arab Emirates leadership, said at a security conference in Bahrain last weekend, the Manama Dialogue hosted by the International Institute for Strategic Studies, or IISS. "We are clearly seeking a political off-ramp."

Qatar has been trying to negotiate between the US and Iran but has also been conducting emergency drills at its airport. The Emiratis have been doing similar drills. And Saudi Arabia is looking at a war on two fronts: The oil-rich state is already under attack from the Houthi militia in neighboring Yemen. Even Egypt has reacted, reportedly strengthening air defenses around the Suez Canal.

Still, no matter what happens next, the US-Israel-Iran war has already made Gulf states evaluate defense partnerships with the US. Rather than making them safe, US bases have made them a target.

"There is widespread disappointment across the region with the US over the folly of the war, the erratic nature of Washington's military and diplomatic conduct, and its decision to prioritize Israel's defense over that of its Gulf partners," Hasan Alhasan, a senior fellow at the IISS, told news agency Bloomberg this week. "If the US liberation of Kuwait in 1991 was the moment that established America's role in the region, the 2026 war may be remembered as the moment that undid it."

Gulf states are widening their outreach with, for example, the UAE getting closer to Israel and the Saudis entering the Mecca defense pact with Pakistan and Turkey.

"The Gulf states have already moved toward strategic autonomy and self-reliance," Andreas Krieg, a senior lecturer at the School of Security Studies at King's College London, wrote for online outlet Middle East Eye this week. "A lame-duck Trump will speed that process."

That's even though a Democrat-led Congress might make the US a slightly more reliable partner again.

Krieg argues that Gulf states should come together to pressure the US not to attack Iran and then negotiate a non-aggression pact with Iran — without US involvement.

Whether that kind of unity is possible in the Gulf is dubious, Ryan Crocker, a former US ambassador to the region and distinguished fellow with MEI, replied to a question from DW during the MEI's online briefing.

"I'm afraid that meaningful Gulf unity is not coming to a theater near us anytime soon," Crocker said. The Qataris remain untrusting because of how other Gulf states acted against them in 2017. And, he added, "there are well known differences between Saudi Arabia and the UAE we see in Sudan [and] in Yemen, and, of course, the profound differences … on relations with Israel. So I would not expect any meaningful moves toward [Gulf] security cooperation."

What's more likely in the long term is Gulf states eventually coming to some kind of understanding with Iran.

"The Gulf states understand that they probably can't buy their security only from Washington, and that they're going to have to buy it, to some degree, by some kind of a relationship with Tehran," the MEI's Harrison added. "It's inevitable. Geography reigns."

What that sort of non-aggression pact looks like remains to be seen, he added. That's why one of the big concerns for Gulf leaders now, is the conflict escalating again to a point where the path back to some sort of diplomacy is lost, Harrison concluded.

#153. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#151. Slashing number of police forces in England and Wales is only option, says Met chief

Mark Rowley calls for ‘revolution’ in policing as major review is expected to propose regional ‘super forces’

Police are heading for failure unless the number of forces in England and Wales is slashed, with their ability to catch criminals collapsing and public confidence falling, Britain’s top officer will say on Friday.

The stark comments from Sir Mark Rowley, the commissioner of the Metropolitan police, come as some police leaders demand a radical package of reforms.

An official review ordered by the government has concluded that the number of police forces in England should be cut from 39 to as few as nine “super forces”, according to multiple sources.

The review, carried out by the former Met police commissioner Bernard Hogan-Howe, is yet to be published.

Sources say that under consideration is the establishment of nine regional forces, plus a single force for Wales, which would sit above more than 150 local policing units that would deliver most neighbourhood crime-fighting activities.

The axing of most of the 43 forces in England and Wales is seen as essential by the Hogan-Howe review and some police chiefs. Those chiefs, including Rowley, are concerned that the government may delay the most radical change to policing in seven decades.

Hogan-Howe’s review was completed in September and sent to the home secretary, Shabana Mahmood. Government sources say only that it will be published before Christmas, with a white paper due in the new year.

The delay is understood to be because of Andy Burnham’s devolution agenda and desire to “rewire” the British state and reform local government, including adding more mayors.

One police source said: “There are some concerns Andy Burnham may want to align the new police structures with mayors, which could mean 20 or more forces. That is not a radical enough change.”

Rowley will speak at an event in Manchester on Friday where he and other chiefs including Greater Manchester’s chief constable, Sir Stephen Watson, will press for a reduction in the number of forces.

The event’s title is “The cost of standing still is that criminals will win”. Some other chief constables have told the Guardian they are annoyed by the Met-organised event and are sceptical or do not support the big reduction down to nine forces.

Cutting the number of forces in England and Wales is the centrepiece of Mahmood’s radical changes to policing. Hogan-Howe’s report, multiple sources say, gives a range of how many larger forces there should be in the new policing landscape, from nine to 18.

Rowley will say in his speech that public confidence has already fallen and that positive outcomes – that is, crimes detected by officers that lead to a charge, caution, out of court settlement or no further action – “are now less than half what they were 12 years ago”.

According to extracts released before the speech, Rowley will say: “I believe this is a choice between the third greatest revolution in British policing or the inevitable decline and fewer bobbies on the beat.”

He will say the 43 forces in England and Wales should be cut to eight or 10, and add: “Policing has tried to keep up, made do as best it can to patch over our broken model … But the model is creaking. We have 43 overstretched forces trying to do too much with too little.”

Hogan-Howe believes the changes would free up policing to rapidly innovate as new crime types and threats emerge. The 43 forces model, overseen by 43 police and crime commissioners, is accused of being too slow and delivering a postcode lottery of varying service for crime victims.

Hogan-Howe came out publicly in favour of a reduction to nine forces 12 years ago.

The bigger forces would handle major crimes – such as murders and serious sexual violence – and public order, as well as expensive but essential back-office functions such as human resources, currently done by each of the 43 forces individually.

Above the regional forces would sit a new nationwide force called the National Police Service, which the government wants to be the home of counter-terrorism policing and serious and organised crime. The advert for a commissioner for the national force is expected to be issued early in the new year.

There is division among police leaders about the big reduction in forces. Some favour voluntary mergers, and the new white paper will allow the mass abolition of forces and creation of new ones to be enforced.

The changes could lead to a vast south-east England force stretching from the London borders to the southern coast of England. A new north-west force would extend from the Mersey to the Pennines, covering Greater Manchester, Merseyside and Lancashire, as well as swallowing up smaller forces such as Cheshire and Cumbria.

One chief constable told the Guardian: “Everyone knows 43 forces have to go. But the return on investment could take five or six years.”

Another chief constable said: “If I was the chief constable of the super force, it would be such a huge area, I wouldn’t know anybody. I worry about it being too big.”

Matthew Scott, the Conservative police and crime commissioner for Kent, said: “They will spend a great deal of time tearing up the fabric of policing for a suboptimal outcome.” The government plans to abolish PCCs as part of its changes.

Another police leader asked: “Where is the evidence bigger forces are better?” They cited the fact that in recent years the largest – such as the Met, Greater Manchester and West Midlands – had failed so badly they had been put into special measures and their chief constables or commissioners had been ousted.

Scotland and Northern Ireland are each already covered by a single force.

#150. Gavin Newsom commutes sentences of all death row inmates in California

Gavin Newsom, a Democrat and a rumored presidential candidate, moved to commute the death sentences of 548 inmates in California for one of his last acts as governor of the state.

California Governor Gavin Newsom said Thursday that he will change all death sentences in the state to life in prison without the possibility of parole.

"I take this action because I firmly believe that state-sanctioned killing is incompatible with California's values, undermines the foundations of our legal system, and diminishes each of us," Newsom said in a letter to Californians.

There are 548 people on death row in California.

The outgoing governor added in the letter that he has long opposed the death penalty and issued a moratorium on executions in 2019.

The California state constitution requires the California Supreme Court's approval to grant clemency to anyone convicted of a felony more than once, which means the court would have to approve 382 of those commutations, or roughly 70%, according to numbers provided by Newsom's office.

Newsom's announcement also comes after the failed execution of Christa Pike renewed scrutiny of the death penalty.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Newsom, who is rumored to be considering a run for president in 2028, said the decision was among the most difficult choices of his career because it will surface painful memories for crime victims' families.

"As Governor, I alone bear responsibility for this decision and its impact, just as I alone would bear responsibility for each execution," Newsom wrote.

He acknowledged that Californians had voted to keep the death penalty. In 2016, 53% of people rejected a ballot measure that would have abolished the death penalty in the state.

"But the constitution also gave every governor clemency authority, and that power exists for moments when law as applied cannot be squared with justice," Newsom said. "For me, this is that moment."

The American Civil Liberties Union (ACLU), who has said the implementation of the death penalty reflects racial bias, applauded the announcement.

"These commutations reflect what justice demands: an end to a racist, error-prone, and inhumane punishment that has burdened California for far too long," said Anthony Romero, the executive director of ACLU.

In 2025, 17 countries carried out a total of at least 2,707 executions, according to Amnesty International.

#145. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#142. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#141. Myanmar’s Aung San Suu Kyi ‘sounds strong’ but is isolated, son says

Kim Aris tells the Guardian chance to speak with detained Nobel laureate was ‘public relations’ move from military leaders

Myanmar’s former democratic leader Aung San Suu Kyi appears to be in good physical and mental health but is living in near-complete isolation, her son has said after the first call with his 81-year-old mother in more than five years.

“She still sounds certainly all there mentally, and physically she sounds strong,” Kim Aris said. “I don’t think she has anybody outside of the military or the police around her, so essentially she’s been held in solitary confinement from what I gather.”

After being deposed in a 2021 military coup, the Nobel Peace prize laureate has been moved to an unspecified location in the capital. “I’m still not entirely sure where she is,” said Aris. “I think it’s a residence in Naypyidaw, which is fenced off and it has a scrubby bit of land around it.”

Aris, who has lived in the UK most of his life and whose late father was a British historian, was allowed to speak to his mother on Thursday at the Myanmar embassy in London. While he welcomed the chance to talk, he said the invitation was part of a “public relations” campaign by the country’s military leaders.

The former junta chief Min Aung Hlaing has sought to rebuild international diplomatic ties, and in April switched his military fatigues for civilian clothes after elections that were widely condemned as a sham.

Activists have long demanded “proof of life” for Aung San Suu Kyi, as well as scores of other political prisoners. In August, she was finally photographed during a Red Cross visit. Last week, a government spokesperson published images of her smiling in a wood-panelled room as officials delivered two gardening books given to her as gifts by Britain’s King Charles. A paper calendar on the wall behind her showed the date.

Aris said these photo-ops were well-known stunts that the regime had used before to pretend his mother, a national icon, was being treated fairly. The daughter of the assassinated national independence hero Gen Aung San has spent much of her life since the late 1980s in and out of house arrest for leading pro-democracy movements.

“She remains completely shut off from the rest of the world. I think she’s unaware of what’s happening outside of where she is because she’s not allowed any newspapers or radio or television,” Aris said. “She’s been locked up for five years on trumped-up, politically motivated charges and she should be free.”

Ruled for decades by generals and suffering from western sanctions, Myanmar experienced a brief democratic moment after Aung San Suu Kyi was allowed to run in a 2015 election that her party overwhelmingly won. She took up the role of “state counsellor” under a civilian-military power-sharing agreement.

Her international reputation was soon tarnished after the 2016-2017 mass killings and expulsions of Rohingya Muslims. Aung San Suu Kyi flew to the international court of justice in The Hague, where she said she acknowledged military units might have used “disproportionate force”, but defended the government against accusations of genocide.

Aris said that his mother was never “silent” on the Rohingya crisis and what she said on the issue “could fill volumes”. Under her leadership, Myanmar launched a domestic inquiry and appointed the former UN chief Kofi Annan to head a commission.

He added that as state counsellor, Aung San Suu Kyi was “always walking on a tightrope”, knowing the military could strip her of her fragile power at any moment.

“The very hard-won nascent democracy that she was trying to lead was holding on to things by a thread,” he said. “The military could have taken over at any point, and they were doing their best to make my mother’s situation a lot more difficult and to undermine her position. And I think in that respect they did a pretty good job.”

The 2021 power grab by the military triggered a civil war that has killed more than 100,000 people, according to conflict monitors.

Aris said the level of public global support for his mother had “dwindled incredibly” since the Rohingya crisis and that the whole country was now suffering from a lack of international attention. While many governments have called for Aung San Suu Kyi’s release, she is not treated as the beloved international figure that she once was.

“Never mind my mother, the fact that people ignore what’s happening in Burma shouldn’t be down to what was being done to the Rohingya,” he said, using the country’s former name. “And that seems to be the case sometimes, that people say: ‘Oh, well, look how the Rohingya were treated. We can ignore what’s happening in Burma.’”

#139. What Marco Rubio’s sidestepping of New Delhi says about the US’s stance on India

Rubio’s decision not to meet Narendra Modi last week was seen by some as a snub – but by focusing on business leaders and oil, the secretary of state made American priorities clear. Plus: the artist bringing a female perspective to men’s clothing

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

When the US secretary of state, Marco Rubio, visited India for the second time this year, headlines concentrated on who he met and where he went.

The fact that Rubio bypassed New Delhi and did not meet the prime minister, Narendra Modi, during his trip last week, and instead traveled to the financial capital, Mumbai, where he met India’s foreign minister, Subrahmanyam Jaishankar (pictured top with Rubio), and business leaders, did not go unnoticed. Nor did his decision to travel to Jamnagar, the small Gujarati town that is home to Reliance Industries’ colossal oil refinery complex.

Bilateral ties between the two countries have come under strain under the second Trump administration, particularly over tariffs and India’s purchase of Russian oil. Yet, Rubio’s return to India just five months after his first visit as secretary of state signals that the country remains useful for president Donald Trump’s ambitions after all.

As director of the South Asia Program at the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace, Milan Vaishnav has spent a decade tracking India’s place in the world, particularly its ties with the US. Vaishnav has described Trump 2.0 as “unbound” marking a new era in India-US relations.

Tracking foreign affairs, he told me, is a minefield right now. From the White House’s transactional approach to global alliances, ongoing conflicts in the Middle East and consequential elections around the world, politics is changing at a pace that means all predictions are off the table. “I think anyone who tells you they know exactly what is going on and how it fits into their neat theory of the world is lying to you,” says Vaishnav.

I asked him about the many confusing aspects of the US-India relationship, from the hostile political rhetoric to the US embrace of Pakistan, as well as Rubio’s latest visit.

Ahead of Rubio’s visit, several commentators noted the secretary of state’s “unusual” itinerary. Vaishnav thought the pre-visit chatter about Rubio skipping Delhi to visit Mumbai and Jamnagar was overblown. “Not every bilateral meeting has to take place in a national capital,” he says.

But that doesn’t mean it wasn’t noteworthy. “I do think the choice of venues was intentional. This administration and, particularly, this president, sees foreign policy through the lens of ‘deal-making’. And if you’re trying to drum up business deals, what better place to visit than Mumbai?” he says.

The Jamnagar visit, where Rubio met Mukesh Ambani, the chair of Reliance, also indicates some politicking. “At a time when petrol prices are extremely high in the United States and are likely to be a major issue in the 3 November midterm elections, the administration wants to show that it is doing everything it can to bring them down. India is a major consumer of oil, but it is also a major exporter of refined petroleum products,” says Vaishnav. “One of Rubio’s objectives was to encourage Reliance and, by extension, India to process more Venezuelan crude [oil],” he added.

While this could present an opportunity for India to advance relations, it’s not a given: the US president remains, after all – and to put it lightly – mercurial.

“The biggest plot twist for me has been the Trump administration’s embrace of Pakistan after the May 2025 conflict with India,” says Vaishnav. Pakistan’s standing in Washington had eroded over the years, especially under Joe Biden, who left office without ever having made a presidential phone call to his counterpart in Pakistan. “Many officials in Congress had grown exhausted with Pakistan’s double game, speaking the language of partnership on the one hand while undermining US interests on the other,” says Vaishnav.

But, after coming to power the second time, Trump thanked the country for its help in nabbing a suspect linked to a 2021 bombing in Afghanistan that killed 13 US service members. This reversal in Pakistan’s fortunes gathered pace after the May conflict with India, which Trump took credit for ending. In June, he welcomed Asim Munir, Pakistan’s army chief, to the White House, after Munir called for the president to be nominated for the Nobel peace prize.

“I chalk up the reversal to a few factors: Trump’s well-known strongman envy, Pakistan’s savvy diplomacy, and the prospect of energy and crypto deals involving members of Trump’s inner circle,” says Vaishnav. For him, it isyet another demonstration of how much weight Trump places on deals, transactions and short-term wins. “For New Delhi, this means Modi’s personal rapport with Trump and 25 years of investment in the bilateral relationship offer no guarantee against a 180-degree reversal in US policy,” he adds.

In a recent piece, Vaishnav’s colleague Sameer Lalwani pointed out that the US relationship with India remains more resilient than the political rhetoric may suggest with quiet, but nonetheless strategic, progress being made.

As Lalwani points out, bilateral trade reached a record high between India and the US in 2025; nearly $68bn worth of AI infrastructure investment in India has been pledged by US technology companies; and the two countries have carried out record number of joint military exercises this year.

Despite these wins, in terms of future bilateral relations, “there are at least three reasons for caution,” warns Vaishnav. First, he points to the onslaught of US actions that have dented Indian interests. Trump had imposed a punitive 50% tariff on India in 2025, which was reduced to 18% earlier this year, but without a comprehensive trade deal being reached. Second, on immigration, the White House’s H-1B visa and green card restrictions have disproportionately affected Indian workers in Silicon Valley.

“This has created a real trust deficit on the Indian side, and with good reason,” says Vaishnav. The frustration was on display earlier this month, when New Delhi branded remarks made by the US vice-president, JD Vance, about Indian professionals as “deeply offensive”. Furthermore, Vaishnav argues that while governments and private sector firms might be great at making announcements, they’re not always a guarantee of delivery.

His final caution is how political tensions can have a chilling effect. The trade negotiations offer a telling example: in January, the US commerce secretary, Howard Lutnick, blamed the lack of a trade deal on Modi’s failure to phone Trump, a characterisation that New Delhi has disputed. Vaishnav says: “Much of the progress we are seeing is the product of decisions made years ago. It is one thing to keep existing projects moving, but turbulence in the relationship could make new initiatives a heavier lift.”

If India is serious about making its ambition to be a global powerhouse a reality, Vaishnav thinks there’s a clearcut way to win. “I would argue that the most important investment is political – ensuring India remains a robust, inclusive, participatory democracy,” he says. “On the economic front, India has made massive new investments in physical and digital infrastructure. But the returns on those investments will be limited unless the government doubles down on reforms: more efficient dispute resolution, an impartial legal system, even-handed tax enforcementand regulatory certainty.”

Ultimately, neither side can afford to treat the other as expendable. The test now is whether strategic foundations can hold at a time of global political upheaval.

A recommendation from someone with great taste

Film-maker Ritu Sarin, director of the annual Dharamshala international film festival, which runs from 29 October to 1 November, suggests a film from this year’s lineup:

“Shot over three years on real locations, Anmol Sidhu’s sophomore feature, Flowers of Acacia, follows wedding orchestra dancers in rural Punjab with a gaze that allows all the large brutalities and smaller intimacies to shine through. Sidhu’s confident, consummate grasp of the medium is on full display in terrifying scenes that stretch to the edge of what is palatable. The camera refuses to look away.”

That’s the number of trees Mumbai’s municipal authorities authorised to be felled over five months this year, to make way for development works, according to the Indian Express. Of these, about 3,500 will be permanently removed, while the rest will be replanted elsewhere.

Prarthna Singh has consistently placed the female identity at the heart of her artistic practice, documenting Indian women’s aspirations and acts of rebellion in male-dominated spaces. When commissioned to photograph rkivecity’s menswear line, made from discarded clothes reworked into new garments, she turned that same gaze outward. The setting was the acclaimed 151-year-old Mayo College boys’ school in Ajmer, Rajasthan – Singh attended the linked girls’ school, and credits it with shaping her rebellious spirit.

“I was thinking about how I could bring a sense of intimacy and camaraderie of boarding-school life to the images, because that exists,” she told me. One image shows a pair of hands holding another boy’s jacket, the faces left out of the frame, so the garment stands in for the familiarity they share. Seeing this work against the backdrop of students taking to the streets to fight for their rights lends the series a lasting significance. “This was just my way of showing all of this is connected. These worlds are not existing separately,” said Singh.
