#96. Why a US-funded vaccine trial on babies was cancelled in Guinea-Bissau

Like many neighbouring countries, Guinea-Bissau waits until babies are six weeks old to vaccinate them against hepatitis B, while the WHO says it should be done at birth (file photo)

A proposed trial of a hepatitis B vaccine on newborn babies in Guinea-Bissau has been cancelled following accusations that the trial was "unethical" and "neocolonial".

The trial was funded by the US, under Health Secretary Robert F Kennedy Jr, who has a long record of expressing scepticism about vaccines.

Police have seized a batch of vaccines from the research centre at the heart of the controversy.

The proposed trial drew international criticism because it planned to withhold a vaccine recommended by the World Health Organization (WHO) from half of the participating newborns.

Some public health experts argued that the proposed trial raised broader questions about whether vulnerable populations in African countries were being asked to take risks that researchers would not accept in wealthier countries.

The BBC has asked the US department of health and human services for comment.

The study was planned at the Bandim Health Project, a research centre that has operated in Guinea-Bissau since 1978.

It was designed to involve 14,000 newborns, with half receiving a hepatitis B vaccine shortly after birth and the other half not being given the shot.

Bandim said the trial aimed to investigate whether the hepatitis B vaccine given at birth has broader health effects beyond preventing infection.

Previous studies in The Gambia and Guinea-Bissau have raised questions about a possible link to increased mortality.

Researchers argued that because Guinea-Bissau does not currently offer routine hepatitis B vaccinations at birth, despite WHO recommendations, they have a unique opportunity to study its effects before such vaccines are introduced in 2028.

But critics questioned the ethics of withholding the vaccine from some babies, particularly in one of the world's poorest countries, where the infection is widespread.

The Trump administration funded the proposed study, with the CDC awarding the University of Southern Denmark (SDU) $1.6m (Â£1.2m) in December 2025.

Walter Kazadi Mulombo, the WHO representative in Guinea-Bissau, told the BBC they became concerned after receiving questions about the study and finding no publicly available protocol.

He said the main ethical concern was that the trial would withhold an intervention already proven to protect newborns against hepatitis B.

"The vaccine has been used for more than 30 years in more than 115 countries," he said. "A study that will withhold that intervention from other newborns is unethical."

He warned that babies could be exposed to the virus during childbirth if their mothers were infected, as well as later in life.

Nearly a fifth of the population of Guinea-Bissau are infected with hepatitis B, a viral infection that affects the liver.

Those infected during infancy are particularly likely to develop chronic infections, which kill up to 25% of people, mostly through liver failure or liver cancer, according to data published in 2020.

The WHO is working with the government to bring forward the birth-dose vaccine.

"The country had been expected to add it to its routine immunisation schedule in 2028, but officials are considering introducing it in the first quarter of next year," Mulombo said.

Guinea-Bissau currently vaccinates children against hepatitis B at six weeks, which means it is less effective.

Guinea-Bissau's government prohibited hepatitis B vaccine trials involving children in January. At the time, it requested a team of experts from the Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention (Africa CDC) to help evaluate the study.

Health Minister Quinhin Nantote said in October that the suspension remained in force and had not been overturned by the Council of Ministers after reports emerged claiming that the trial would be taking place after all.

"We were surprised to learn from media reports that the study might proceed," Nantote said. "We are outraged because even I, as the minister responsible for this sector, was not informed."

The dispute intensified after the SDU said a revised study protocol had received ethical approval "from the relevant authorities in Guinea-Bissau", which Nantote denied.

Dr Gavin Yamey, a professor of global health and public policy at Duke University in the US, criticised the proposal.

"The proposed trial reeks of neocolonialism," he told the BBC, questioning why the research was not being conducted in Denmark.

His criticism centred on the decision to withhold a recommended vaccine from some newborns in a country where hepatitis B is widespread.

SDU dean Jakob Grauslund said the trial had never started, no babies had been enrolled and no vaccinations administered as part of the research.

The SDU said it had received a separate, positive assessment from the university's own research ethics committee.

Grauslund said the university had sought an additional independent assessment, although Danish law did not require one.

He added that the relevant Danish statutory research ethics bodies were unable to undertake the review.

Following the approval from the SDU's ethics committee, Grauslund decided that the project could proceed.

He, however, stressed that this did not replace the separate authorisation required from Guinea-Bissau's authorities.

On 19 October, Guinea-Bissau's Judicial Police searched the Bandim Health Project's facilities in the capital, Bissau, and seized a batch of hepatitis B vaccines.

Police said the batch was intended for vaccinating children but did not disclose how many doses had been seized or the specific grounds for the investigation.

They stressed that the seizure did not, in itself, mean the vaccines were defective or unsafe.

The police have not explicitly said whether their investigation relates to the proposed trial, the importation of the vaccines or other circumstances surrounding the batch.

Grauslund said he could not comment on the seizure of the vaccines.

The proposed trial was funded by the US government at a time of significant changes to vaccine policy under President Donald Trump and Health Secretary Kennedy.

Under Kennedy's leadership, a US vaccine panel attempted to end a longstanding recommendation that all newborns receive a hepatitis B vaccine at birth, but a federal judge later blocked this, along with other changes to vaccine policy made by the Trump administration.

Critics have questioned whether the research could help support that policy shift, although the study's stated aim was to investigate potential effects of the vaccine beyond preventing infection.

Kennedy has praised the Danish researchers as "the deities of vaccine research", and cited earlier findings from their work in support of concerns about vaccine safety. A subsequent study by some of the same researchers, however, found no evidence of the previously reported effect.

There is no evidence that the proposed trial was designed to justify a change in US policy.

#95. Beware ‘sun, sea and surgery’ cosmetic tourism packages, experts advise

British College of Aesthetic Medicine launches checklist as part of ‘vet it before you get it’ campaign

Cosmetic tourism is often sold as a package of sun, sea and surgery, but experts have said travelling overseas for procedures can end in infections, disfigurement and death.

Nora Nugent, a consultant plastic surgeon and former president of the British Association of Aesthetic Plastic Surgeons, said travelling to countries such as Turkey for surgery came with myriad risks – and not just on the operating table.

“Actually, the risks start from the moment you step on the plane to after you return home, and until you’re fully healed and you’re out of the clotting risk time,” she said, noting that travel itself could increase the risks of clots. A lack of in-person aftercare is also a concern.

The British College of Aesthetic Medicine has launched a checklist as part of its “vet it before you get it” campaign, intended to help potential patients make better and safer choices about overseas cosmetic surgery.

As well as setting out red flags to look out for, the campaign suggests those seeking cosmetic surgery independently verify a surgeon’s qualifications, determine what emergency facilities are available near the chosen clinic and find out what insurance the practitioner has in place.

Nugent said a major driver for patients seeking procedures in countries such as Turkey was their relative cheapness, but that prices were high in the UK as a result of factors including the higher cost of living and higher fees for medical indemnity and insurance.

Chrissie Wolfe, the founder and chief executive of Lupa Law, a specialist medical negligence practice, said there could be a host of difficulties in making a claim should a procedure go wrong overseas, and that even if compensation were awarded, it could be far lower than in the UK.

“Turkey is the most popular destination by far for cosmetic surgery, but it is absolutely the most difficult from a legal perspective,” she said. “When something goes wrong, it’s incredibly difficult to put right.”

She said those looking to undergo cosmetic surgery abroad should be wary of clinics selling bundles of procedures or time-limited deals, noting that one patient had gone to Turkey for a tummy tuck but was upsold a “mummy makeover” that included a breast uplift.

“She came back and had a very severe infection,” she said. “She ultimately ended up losing most of her left breast and the entire right breast on a procedure that she didn’t even want in the first place.”

Wolfe said the marketing of travelling abroad for cosmetic surgery as a glossy holiday was problematic. “It shifts the patient’s mind away from the seriousness of what they’re actually embarking on,” she said.

It is not the first time overseas cosmetic surgery has sparked warnings. The then health secretary, Wes Streeting, urged caution over such procedures in 2024.

Data, however, suggests growing numbers of residents in Great Britain are travelling overseas for medical treatment. According to data from the Office for National Statistics, about 524,000 did so in 2024, including cosmetic procedures, up from 431,000 in 2023.

Brazillian butt lifts (BBLs) have the highest risk of death, but other overseas procedures have also left patients with problems.

Nugent cited the case of a woman who had developed abscesses and infections in her neck after a facelift. She needed long-term antibiotics and drainage procedures and was left with significant scarring.

Another woman in her late 20s had a breast lift and labiaplasty abroad after pregnancy. She ended up with a loss of tissue on one breast, her implant exposed and over-reduced labia. As a result she needed corrective surgeries, including to redo the lifts and improve the labiaplasty.

“But you can’t get things back to how they were,” said Nugent.

A third woman underwent a tummy tuck overseas, she said, but the wound came apart and required surgical cleaning. She has been left with scarring and a dip in her tummy.

“These are three examples of patients who suffered fairly horrific complications from procedures that were meant to improve how they looked and how they felt about themselves and have ended up doing exactly the opposite,” Nugent said.

#93. A bitter end for Ronaldo - the icon who transformed Portugal

Cristiano Ronaldo's old rival Leo Messi left Argentina less than a month ago with a nation applauding him.

The Portugal legend has gone another way, and it was always going to be the case.

The 41-year-old announced his international retirement after 23 years but also after a fallout with coach Jorge Jesus which was resolved with a sanction of 30 days, that had already been served, and a fine of 255 euros.

It was the punishment imposed on the Portuguese striker for leaving the team's training camp without permission. Jesus kept him on the bench for one match and said he did not plan to start him in the next, so Ronaldo went home.

His last appearance came on 24 September against Wales. He ends his international career with 146 goals in 234 games.

A message went around Portugal with a big thank you for everything, calling Ronaldo a global ambassador and an inspiration to millions. Even so, the way it ended leaves a bit of a bitter taste.

Was he going to be named in the next squad? Possibly, as Jesus said the door was open and the decision was Ronaldo's. What is clear is that he was no longer an automatic starter, and Cristiano has never handled that well.

So is this another hissy fit? Partly, yes. He reacted the way he always has when a coach questions his place. But the label hides how much he adapted over the years.

Such a pattern appears with almost every man who sat on the Portuguese bench - there were clashes, but also communion with managers and team-mates that help explain this ending.

Six tournaments, 11 goals - Ronaldo's World Cup legacy

Ronaldo's most iconic moments as he retires from international football

What legacy does Cristiano Ronaldo leave on the international stage?

When a young Ronaldo first joined the squad, he arrived at camp with his baseball cap on backwards, enormous sunglasses and headphones turned up so loud they could be heard from a distance.

He would not take the cap off to eat among veterans like Luis Figo and Rui Costa. Luiz Felipe Scolari saw an extraordinary player who was immature and obsessed with showing off. He reprimanded him in public, then told him privately that if he changed his attitude, he would one day captain Portugal.

The first great disappointment came early, and it came at home. At 19, Ronaldo played in the final of Euro 2004 at the Estadio da Luz in Lisbon, in front of a country that had convinced itself the trophy was already on its way.

A single headed goal by Angelos Charisteas was enough for unfancied Greece to win it by surprise. When the final whistle went, Ronaldo could not hold back the tears, and the image of the teenager crying on his own pitch travelled everywhere.

It was a painful way to learn what a final costs, and it left him with a hunger that never really went away.

Figo owned the number 7 at the time, so Ronaldo had to have 17. Figo helped him settle but had no intention of making it easy. When he left the national team in 2006, the shirt and the team became Ronaldo's.

In September 2005, before a qualifier in Russia, Scolari and Figo went to his room to tell him his father had died. Scolari offered to send him home. Ronaldo chose to play, asked his team-mates to behave as normal, and spent time in the dressing room doing ball tricks.

That was his way to cope with the expected but still hugely hurtful passing of his alcoholic dad. He later called Scolari "father". That way the national team had become the place that held him together.

Things went sour with Carlos Queiroz. After the 2010 World Cup exit against Spain, asked to explain a poor performance, Ronaldo told journalists to ask the coach. His influence in the camp had grown so large it changed routines and habits, which explains why the coach felt undermined.

The sociologist Joao Nuno Coelho offers the best lens on how Portugal sees him. In his analysis, the national team became a representation of Portuguese identity and of the country's need to be seen internationally.

In years of economic hardship and a crisis of confidence, a team that won admiration abroad was important beyond football.

Ronaldo stood at the centre of that need and created contradictory feelings. Some Portuguese questioned his individualism and his ego, and not everyone saw themselves in his personality.

Coelho explains criticising him became more difficult as time went by. The country wanted to be proud of him and sometimes felt uneasy at the same time.

The former international Jorge Andrade told me some supporters preferred figures like Figo, Eusebio, Paulo Futre or Rui Costa. Even so, Portugal was proud to have him. And with the titles, the doubts eased.

Under Fernando Santos in 2016, he changed. He accepted playing as a centre forward next to Nani - he had never taken a coach's instructions so naturally.

After the opening draw with Iceland, he told Nani in private that he trusted Santos, and the dressing room began to see a real captain. After missing a penalty against Austria, he barely slept. Santos woke up and found him already awake.

At the training camp in Marcoussis, he took a fan's megaphone and told supporters Portugal had won nothing yet. He was enjoying the leadership role.

In the final in Paris, he left the pitch in tears, injured. He had not had the best tournament, with a muscle complaint that did not let him perform at his best.

Nani took the armband. At half-time Ronaldo pulled himself together to encourage his team-mates, and before extra time, still limping, he told Eder he would score.

But the old instinct never fully disappeared. During extra-time, Ronaldo stood in the technical area giving orders until the fourth official sent him back. Other coaches I consulted found it excessive.

His greatest strength, the need to influence every result, also made limits hard to accept.

When he debuted in 2003, Portugal was still searching for its first major title. Some players mark an era, and others change a national team for good. He belongs to the second group.

That night in July 2016 gave Portugal its first European Championship.

Three years later he scored a hat-trick against Switzerland to put them into the first Nations League final, which Portugal won. In 2025 he lifted a second Nations League at an age when most players have retired.

No one has scored more goals for a national side, and no one has pulled on the Portugal shirt more times.

Under his influence, Portugal stopped being a gifted team and became one that arrives at every tournament expecting to contend, while a whole generation of players came through a dressing room where he decided what the standard was.

Coelho's point holds here as well: a small country found a way to be noticed, and for two decades he was its face.

After 2016 the question around him changed. Fewer asked whether he was selfish, and more asked whether Portugal depended on him too much.

Ronaldo scored 146 goals and won 234 caps for Portugal

In the World Cup in 2018, he scored three against Spain, including a free-kick after 45 attempts in major tournaments, and the team still went out to Uruguay.

After a difficult 2026 World Cup, much was made of him still starting up front when in fact statistically that was his best period.

Under Roberto Martinez, with 28 goals in 37 games, 0.756 per match, improves the 0.624 with Santos.

The federation has announced a special tribute to honour his contribution, and that is necessary as the exit has not left fans room for a celebration they want to be part of.

Not only they grew up with him, he took them to places they had not yet been.

#92. Protesters march in cities across France as unions stage general strike

Hundreds of thousands demonstrate over cost of living and cuts to public services as 70,000 police officers deployed

Hundreds of thousands of people have marched in cities across France in the latest round of street demonstrations, as trade unions put pressure on the government with a general strike over the cost of living and cuts to public services.

Striking teachers, hospital staff and rail workers joined high school and university students in largely peaceful demonstrations in Paris and other cities on Thursday amid a high police presence of 70,000 officers.

The government estimated that at least 600,000 people had taken part, while the leftwing CGT trade union put the figure at almost 1.6 million.

Riot police fired teargas at the end of demonstrations in Bordeaux and Lyon. In Paris, police prevented schoolchildren from setting up barricades with wheelie bins outside high schools across the city in the early morning.

In the east of the city, police used teargas to prevent students from accessing the Hélène Boucher high school.

“This is a historic protest movement but the government’s only real response has been to seriously crack down on it with police, so we had to come out on to the street today,” said Rebecca, 30, a social worker for children with disabilities from Paris’s southern suburbs. “There are police everywhere, but where is the funding for other public services?”

Emmanuel Macron’s final months in office continue to be marred by domestic crises, as high school students protesting over poor learning conditions have been joined by workers in weekly protests.

Meanwhile, the government is struggling to pass a belt-tightening budget in a divided parliament as public debt approaches 120% of economic output.

France’s four main rail unions said the government’s proposed budget cuts would “worsen poverty, slash social security, freeze state workers’ salaries and dry up investment needed for environmental measures”.

Amid the renewed protests centred on living costs, public spending cuts and the troubled education system, the far-right, anti-immigration presidential candidate Marine Le Pen is polling high for the 2027 election to replace Macron next spring.

High school students, who have been protesting for weeks, continued to blockade schools over overcrowded classes and dilapidated buildings, saying the government’s proposed measures on capping some class sizes and loaning funds to regional authorities to fix schools were not enough. The government said 81 high schools were affected on Thursday, while students’ unions put the number at 1,000.

University students joined in blockading faculties, with a third of higher education sites affected, the government said. Trade unions from across the political landscape joined strikes, with private school teacher unions taking part alongside state school teachers.

At the Paris demonstration, a 46-year-old psychiatric nurse from the city’s south-eastern suburbs said: “We’ve got to protect public services. Hospital cuts are impacting the work we try to do with psychiatric patients.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

“There are fewer doctors and nurses. People are overworked and fatigued. It’s hard to make ends meet on salaries that haven’t properly been reviewed for years.”

A Paris train driver with the CGT union said: “It’s important that we also come out to demonstrate against the rise of the far right as the presidential election approaches.”

Rights groups protested over what they said was heavy-handed policing and an attempt by Macron’s executive to clamp down on the right to demonstrate.

The Ligue des droits de l’Homme (French human rights league) said this week there had been an “authoritarian shift” to stifle “any dissenting voice”, as authorities issued restrictions on demonstrating in city centres as well as curfews in certain areas.

Sophie Binet, the head of the CGT union, said: “We’re living at a very serious time, with a very dangerous authoritarian drift from the government.” She criticised decisions by authorities in some cities to push demonstrations out of city centres.

As the protests were under way, the prime minister, Sébastien Lecornu, announced a meeting to be held next Monday with trade unions and business leaders to discuss salaries and the cost of living.

#90. Would you take a test to see if your life will be cut short? I did - and it changed me

Laura Sides faced a dilemma of whether to get tested to see if she had inherited her father's condition

If you could find out if you had a gene that would cut your life short, would you?

This is the dilemma Laura Sides faced when she was offered a test for the rare form of early onset dementia that her dad was diagnosed with in his 40s.

Laura spoke to Dr Sian Williams, host of BBC Radio 4's Life Changing podcast, about her decision to take the test and how it has shaped the rest of her life.

Growing up, Laura says she always felt close to her dad as they had a "similar nature". He was a GP and "the most amazing doting father," she says. When his Alzheimer's symptoms became more severe, Laura dropped out of university to care for him.

"We did have a really lovely time," she says, remembering trips to a lighthouse and collecting coasters. "I'd run him baths and just play music and pour jugs of hot water over his head and he loved it."

Laura had just celebrated her 21st birthday when she began caring for her dad

It was at a hospital appointment with her dad when a doctor told Laura she had a 50/50 chance of developing the same type of early onset Alzheimer's known as the "London variant".

"All I could think was, that's a toss of a coin," she says. "I was still so involved with what was going on with him [her dad]. I just pushed it really, really deep down."

Laura's dad died in 2009, just a year after her mum had died.

"All of a sudden I got such an overwhelming urge to leave," she says. So she booked a solo around-the-world trip, eventually settling in Melbourne, Australia.

It was there that thoughts of inheriting her dad's condition "started creeping in" and she went to see a specialist in 2013.

He told her a test could determine with 99.999% certainty if she would inherit early onset dementia. If she had the gene, her expected age of decline would be 46 to 48, he said.

"I just thought, I'm not there yet. I don't want to do that."

'Toss of a coinâ if Iâd inherit dementia gene

As Laura entered her 30s she found herself thinking about the test more and more and decided she wanted to know. But first, she had to undergo six months of genetic counselling.

"There's not many realms in life where you find out your fate and it can be very difficult information to process," she says.

After a six-week wait for the test results to come through, the day finally arrived. Laura says she felt "extremely nervous".

She had booked a table for lunch with a friend afterwards - but went into the appointment alone.

"I said, 'Let's cut straight to it.' They said, 'We're really sorry, it's not good news, and you have inherited the gene.'"

She remembers "almost a white blurry noise of just sitting there".

"I came out of that room and my friend burst into hysterical tears." But Laura didn't react. "We went for our lunch and it was lovely. I don't know if it'd sunk in."

The reality hit later. "I was angry. I felt it was so unjust. I was just too confused at how I was meant to live now. How do you live with that knowledge?"

Laura has taken part in medical trials all over the world

Laura began to feel a pull back home to the UK, especially as her sister was pregnant. "It doesn't make sense... to live on the other side of the world when she's going to have a baby."

However, her around-the-world travels continued as she took part in medical trials before and after her diagnosis.

"It felt like such a positive to be involved in something out of such a negative situation."

Laura (right) is a chef and owns a cafe restaurant and catering business with two friends

Laura is now 43 - three years away from the age doctors predicted she will begin to notice symptoms.

"I keep waiting for something to sort of hit home. I guess one day I'll start declining but that doesn't keep me up at night."

She is "grateful and so lucky for the people in her life". But meeting anyone new is hard.

"Do I tell them? Do I not tell them? At what point do I tell them?

"And more often than not, when I have told them, it ends."

However she says she has been "proven wrong".

"I have been shown I can be loved despite this. It fills my heart with reassurance and joy".

Laura says she had always "naively assumed" she would be a mother.

"[Its] one of the very few things that I never questioned that I'd be good at and I wanted to do. That's hard... but it doesn't make sense to do it. It wouldn't be fair."

Laura loves swimming and is aiming to swim in as many seas as possible

For Laura, life is now about tapping in to things that "make her happy".

"It's not about huge aspirations. It's not like I want to see the Grand Canyon... I want to have time now around the people that I adore and make me feel safe.

"I love nature. I love being outside. I'm obsessed with being submerged in water. It sounds so corny, but...I believe it really is actually the little things are the big things.

"I hope I just have a wonderful few years in this world... drinking champagne and eating oysters - two of my favourite things."

If you or someone you know has been affected by dementia, you can access support from the BBC Action Line.

There is no intelligence yet pointing to any particular group or state actor, such as Russia or Iran, amid worries over hybrid incidents targeting Europe (15:57).

Male State is a Russian far-right, ultranationalist and misogynistic movement founded in 2016 by Vladislav Pozdnyakov, initially as an online community on the social network VKontakte.

Its supporters became known for coordinating harassment campaigns against women, LGBTQ+ people and businesses that had spoken out against discrimination.

In October 2021, the Nizhny Novgorod regional court designated Male State an extremist organisation and banned its activities in Russia.

Pozdnyakov has continued to publish far-right material online and is a prominent supporter of Russia’s war in Ukraine.

On his Telegram channel, Pozdnyakov has neither commented on nor claimed responsibility for the bomb threats.

After reports from Bulgaria (17:37) and Italy (14:38) alleging links with the “Fighters of the Male State,” let’s cross to our Russia expert Pjotr Sauer for a quick explanation on the group – with big caveats that we do not really know for sure if they are involved or not.

A separate Ryanair flight from Pescara in Italy to Brussels Charleroi had to inspected after the word “bomb” was discovered scrawled on the walls of its onboard lavatory.

A Ryanair flight from London Stansted to Cork airport was also held up on arrival in Ireland this morning after a false bomb threat.

“Elevated additional secutity measures were adopted in conjunction with An Garda Síochána. Nothing of concern was identified and the flight resumed operations with a 90-minute delay. The incident has been fully stood down.”

The airport did not identify the flight, but it was named in Irish media as FR901 from London Stansted, which experienced a delay meeting the airport’s description.

This brings today’s total up to 22 and 30 for this week.

A second LOT Polish Airlines flight – this time LO331 from Warsaw Okęcie to Paris - was also caught up in today’s false bomb alerts.

Following an anonymous report, the plane was forced to stay in Warsaw for additional checks, which showed no reason for concern.

It eventually departed just over four hours late and landed safely in Paris in the early afternoon, the Guardian confirmed.

An email sent to Sofia airport during a series of hoax alerts across Europe today was signed “the fighters of the Male State,” AFP reported.

“We, the fighters of the Male State, will make history with our actions today. Your feminist regime will remember this forever. The explosion will occur in 60 minutes. Today, your skies will turn red.”

As reported earlier (14:38), similar messages were also sent to Italian airports.

While 20 false bomb threats across Europe will certainly raise concern, it is worth keeping in mind that this is just a fraction of the overall number of planes flying across the continent every day.

Some 27,400 flights were scheduled in Europe today, according to the European air traffic control agency, Eurocontrol.

The pilot of an Aer Lingus flight from Dublin to Rome that diverted to Évreux following a bomb threat (9:55, 10:07, 10:25) is being questioned by French authorities for initially refusing to land at a military base, according to media reports in France.

AFP, France24, Le Figaro and TF1 said that the pilot reportedly insisted on landing at a civilian airport, such as Beauvais or Nantes, prompting the French air force to intercept the plane with fighter jets.

The four flights affected at Prague airport can now be confirmed as inbound flights from Athens, Dublin, Glasgow and Newcastle (11:16, 11:58).

A spokesperson for the Prague airport, Jiří Hannich, told the Guardian that “an anonymous threat was received concerning the possible presence of explosive devices on board selected flights” arriving to Prague.

“In accordance with the Airport Emergency Plan, the Security Operations Command Center was activated under the direction of the Police of the Czech Republic.

The aircraft landed safely in Prague and were directed to remote stands.

Following their arrival, the Police of the Czech Republic conducted security searches of the aircraft for explosives, all with negative results. All passengers safely disembarked from the aircraft and received their baggage once all necessary security measures had been completed.

The situation had no significant impact on other airport operations. The threat is currently under police investigation. For this reason, we are not in a position to provide any further details.”

Global airlines body Iata has “condemned … attempts to interrupt air transport,” urging the relevant authorities to “find the origins of these hoax threats.”

“Safety is the number one priority for the industry and we take all threats seriously,” Iata’s senior vicepresident for operations, safety and security, Nick Careen said in a statement.

“Meanwhile passengers should be assured that aviation security measures will continue to ensure the highest levels of public safety,” he added.

Austrian defence ministry spokesperson Michael Bauer has confirmed that all affected flights in Austrian airspace were escorted by Eurofighters jets.

“What the Eurofighters can achieve in this scenario: They can observe the situation on site as the sole eyewitness and report any possible deviation from the flight path if the pilot has no radio connection.

If the aircraft is damaged but remains airworthy, the EF pilot can inspect the damage and provide assistance with information about the condition. Through visual contact with the pilot, they can at least support them in any case.”

In a separate post, he confirmed there were three affected flights, which checks out with our tally (11:01).

#83. Bomb threats disrupt dozens of flights across Europe, raising hybrid war fears

Planes forced into emergency landings or diversions two days after hoax alerts affected air travel

Why have more than 30 European flights been hit by bomb threats this week?

Hoax bomb threats have disrupted at least 34 flights across Europe this week, with some forced to make emergency landings, as questions mount over an apparently coordinated effort to cause chaos for travellers.

At least 25 bomb threats affected flights on Thursday between Italy, Ireland, the UK, Spain, Bulgaria, Romania, Poland, Finland, Austria, Denmark, Belgium, Portugal, France, Greece, Serbia and Czechia.

The threats affected flights by Ryanair, Aer Lingus, Wizz Air, Finnair, easyJet, Aegean Airlines, Iberia, Sky Express, Air Serbia and LOT Polish Airlines. All landed safely. Searches of the planes found nothing suspicious.

In the UK, Heathrow, London’s principal airport, and Birmingham airport received threats overnight, but it was determined that they were hoaxes with the help of specialist investigators from the Metropolitan police’s aviation police command.

The Met said threats had been made against four aircraft departing from or arriving at Heathrow. A police spokesperson added that, after an assessment, “no security issues [were] identified”.

The day of hoaxes followed similar threats on Tuesday, when seven planes across Europe were searched or diverted after malicious alerts. On Monday a flight from the UK to Bulgaria was diverted after a threat that was apparently without substance.

Investigators said on Thursday there remained no evidence that the hoaxes were being orchestrated by a hostile terror group or state actor, such as Russia or Iran, although inquiries across Europe into the incidents continue.

The global airlines body Iata condemned the disruption and urged authorities to identify the origin of the threats. “Safety is the number one priority for the industry and we take all threats seriously,” said Nick Careen, Iata’s senior vice-president for operations, safety and security, in a statement.

The EU transport commissioner, Apostolos Tzitzikostas, said it was too early to know if the incidents were linked but that any threats against civilians were unacceptable.

Bomb threats emailed to airports in Rome and Sofia were signed “fighters of the Male State”, according to sources at Aeroporti di Roma, which manages the city’s two airports, and a text seen by Agence France-Presse.

Male State is a Russian far-right, ultranationalist and misogynistic movement founded in 2016 by Vladislav Pozdnyakov. On his Telegram channel Pozdnyakov, a prominent supporter of the war in Ukraine, did not comment on or claim responsibility for the bomb threats.

The emails reportedly read: “We, the fighters of the Male State, will make history with our actions today. Your feminist regime will remember this for ever. The explosion will occur in 60 minutes. Today, your skies will turn red.”

A Cork airport spokesperson confirmed to the Guardian that the airport had received an anonymous threat by phone on Thursday morning, causing a flight to be delayed. It was understood to be a Ryanair flight from London Stansted.

A separate Ryanair flight from Pescara in Italy to Brussels-Charleroi was inspected after the word “bomb” was discovered scrawled on the walls of its onboard toilet.

The affected flights had knock-on effects but were a tiny fraction of the 27,400 flights scheduled in Europe on Thursday, according to the European air traffic control agency, Eurocontrol.

The pilot of an Aer Lingus flight from Dublin to Rome that diverted to Évreux was being questioned by French authorities for initially refusing to land at a military base, according to French media reports.

The disruption followed cyber-attacks this week against supermarket refrigeration systems.

A Kremlin spokesperson said on Thursday it was “absurd” to blame Vladimir Putin’s administration for the fridge hacks. “We have ​such baseless accusations against our country, implicating it in everything,” Dmitry Peskov told reporters.

In Rome, Guido Crosetto, the Italian defence minister, said: “It seems to me that every day now, from refrigerators to blood supplies in France, to these fake alarms, there’s someone who has an interest in stoking tensions.”

Asked if he knew who was behind the hoaxes, he said: “It’s called ‘under-threshold warfare’ because it’s below the attribution threshold. It’s done that way on purpose, so no one can pin the blame.”

Many of the affected flights were operated by low-cost carriers but there was little discernible pattern surrounding the incidents.

Aviation experts say fake threats occur daily but that such a number of credible threats, even if malicious hoaxes, appears to point to coordinated action.

While getting a large explosive onboard should be ruled out by multiple layers of security and screening, the effects of any detonation would be multiplied for planes at typical cruising altitude of 35,000ft.

Diversions are expensive as well as highly disruptive, but airlines have little choice in the face of any remotely credible threat.

The ready availability of airline operations information on the internet means that any hoaxer now has a level of detail about the schedules, the type of plane and its technical specifications that would have eluded all but insiders decades ago.

Rhys Jones, an analyst at Osprey Flight Solutions, an aviation intelligence firm, said the confidence of airlines in European airport security should not mean any change to normal protocols after the hoaxes, but “one of the natural responses will be to see double-downed security, media coverage and public concern about travelling”.

He said the threats seemed linked but “a reasonably chaotic campaign with no particular region targeted”, and while they could weaken future security responses through the normalisation of threats, simply creating disruption could be regarded as a success by those carrying out the hoaxes.

Additional reporting by Pjotr Sauer in Amsterdam and Angela Giuffrida in Rome

#81. Vance walks Trump tightrope with scathing election critique blaming Iran war

With Congress flipped, what does Donald Trump do next?

Vice-President JD Vance sent shockwaves through Washington on Wednesday by blaming his party's midterm defeat squarely on the Iran war, in what was widely interpreted as an effort to distance himself from President Donald Trump ahead of a potential 2028 White House run.

In a carefully calibrated, 600-word statement on X, the vice-president acknowledged that Trump's 2024 voters had deserted Republicans in the midterm election.

"The reason we lost is the war," he wrote. "The president himself said so last night."

In interviews late Tuesday night and Wednesday, Trump had defended the war but acknowledged that it played a factor in Democrats flipping control of the US House of Representatives and the Senate.

Vance's view on the war in Iran is an open secret in Washington. While he has repeatedly backed the conflict since the US and Israel attacked Tehran in February, he has reportedly expressed opposition in private meetings with administration officials.

But those disagreements had been kept out of the public eye - until now.

Could Democrats in Congress end the war in Iran?

Hours after Trump and the party were handed a bruising election defeat, Vance made clear that he felt the decision to go to war was a clear political mistake.

"If you'd like to argue it was worth it, that losing the Senate for the next 6 years was worth it, then do so. Argue it was a worthwhile cost to pay. Agree or disagree, that's at least honest," Vance said.

It was a striking statement, and one with no recent precedent: it's hard to recall a similar moment in recent US political history when a sitting vice-president has so sharply broken with the president on a signature foreign policy issue.

Then came an arguably even greater surprise: Trump - who typically demands steadfast loyalty from his senior advisers - agreed with Vance that the midterms were "largely" decided by the war. He added that he would "do it again" if given the same choices.

BOWEN: Trump's loss limits powers at home, but it will likely be different abroad

THE BIG MOMENTS: Ecstatic scenes that greeted key results

ZURCHER: Midterm carnage weakens Trump but he still wields power for now

'A DISASTER': Angry Republicans blame Trump for midterms defeat

It was an early sign that Trump - who has prized loyalty from his advisers - may give Vance a wide berth should he choose to launch a presidential campaign.

"I know how he feels about it. I think it was -- I think it was fine," Trump told reporters when asked about Vance's post blaming the war. "I knew that when I went in, and we stopped them from having a nuclear weapon, and they will never have a nuclear weapon."

Vance's statement seemed like "a way of separating himself from Trump," said Daniel Byman, a foreign policy expert at the Center for Strategic and International Studies.

Drawing contrasts with Trump will be a delicate balancing act if Vance seeks the presidency, Byman added.

That balance was on display in Vance's post. The vice president didn't mention Trump by name and attributed much of his election results analysis to unnamed pollsters.

Vance "put his toe in the water to see what he's going to do," Ashley Davis, a Republican strategist, told the BBC.

Beyond the Iran war criticism, Vance also offered a political roadmap that could preview a possible presidential campaign. He distilled the party's strategy to win back voters into a neat four-point gameplan: "1) End the war 2) Focus on cost of living/inflation 3) Put America First 4) Change our relationship to Israel."

The list encapsulated much of Vance's worldview since bursting onto the national political scene, and read like the core argument in a future stump speech.

The BBC has contacted Vance's office for comment.

Of course, the next presidential election is still two years away. Vance is expected to run but hasn't made an official decision, and aides insist that he's still mulling it over.

It's also up for debate exactly how much Vance broke from Trump. Vance showed the message to Trump before posting it online, according to The New York Times.

Trump, for his part, could change his mind and make clear he'll brook no dissent from Vance or anyone else.

The president has been circumspect about who he'd support to succeed him as the 2028 Republican presidential nominee. Trump has praised Vance and Secretary of State Marco Rubio on several occasions, but hasn't said when - or if â he'd make an endorsement.

The president appears to relish the guessing game of who he'd back to carry on his political movement, even in the past floating the idea that he'd continue to a third term (which is barred by the US Constitution).

Vance's midterms missive might not be that significant in the end. Or it could have been the starting gun for the 2028 race and the battle for the post-Trump Republican Party.

#80. Liberal bus crashes into shop awning on its first outing of Victorian election campaign

Opposition leader Jess Wilson grimaced as the ‘Jess Express’, carrying Liberals and reporters, backs into the structure in suburban Melbourne

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The Victorian Liberal party’s first offical day on the campaign trail has hit a snag, after its campaign bus reversed into the awning of a shopfront.

The “Jess Express”, a large blue bus emblazoned with an image of the opposition leader, Jess Wilson, was unveiled on Friday, 12 days after Labor launched its own red equivalent.

Featuring Wilson’s slogan, “A Fresh Start for Victoria”, the bus also carries a warning to motorists on its rear: “Dodging Labor’s potholes.”

But it wasn’t Labor’s potholes or political spin that brought the bus undone.

Arriving at Centre Road in Bentleigh for a street walk with Liberal candidate Tynan Putt, the bus was attempting to back into a parking spot when it struck the awning of a jewellery store, damaging the structure. The store was closed at the time.

Wilson grimaced as the incident unfolded, before quipping that the store’s owners wouldn’t have trouble finding the culprit, given her face was plastered across the side of the bus.

The Liberal campaign promptly contacted the store’s owners to provide its details, while the bus escaped with minor scratches. No one on board was injured.

Just hours earlier, its passengers – made up of party members and reporters – read safety cards offering a “guide to surviving 12 years of Labor”.

Instructions included “please remain seated and brace for potholes”, “remain calm” in the event of a “financial emergency” and a warning that passengers may experience “motion sickness” due to “Labor’s constant spin”.

“Please look towards the fresh start on the horizon,” the cards read.

It’s not the first time a campaign bus has caused a political headache. During the final week of the 2025 federal election campaign, Peter Dutton’s bus became stuck after mounting a raised concrete divider and bike lane barrier on Pitt Street in the Sydney CBD.

The bus was stuck for about 40 minutes, causing traffic diversions and delaying the opposition leader’s schedule.

At the 2023 New South Wales state election, the then opposition leader, Chris Minns opted for a zero-emissions bus that broke down after hitting a speed bump in western Sydney.

“I forgot to charge it last night and as a result it broke down,” Minns said at the time.

Journalists were promptly carted into a replacement, petrol-powered, bus.

In 2022, the Liberals in Victoria didn’t opt for a bus, instead debuting their “Ditch Danmobile” – a 1970s-style ambulance emblazoned with slogans.

Within an hour, the head of the Victorian Ambulance Union, questioned whether the stunt was legal, given state laws banning use of the word “ambulance” on any vehicle not owned or operated by an ambulance service without permission from the health department.

Current polling suggests the Coalition is well ahead of Labor, with the latest Newspoll putting it ahead 55-45 on a two-party-preferred basis.

The Liberals have Bentleigh firmly in its sights, despite Labor MP Nick Staikos holding the seat on an 8% margin. The electorate is considered a bellwether, having been won by the party forming government at 14 of the 16 elections since it was first contested in 1967.

Putt said the main issues in the electorate were cost of living and crime.

Victorians go to the polls on 28 November.

#78. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

#76. Delhi's toxic winter air is back - is the Indian capital prepared for pollution?

People exercise in a park in Delhi on Thursday, breathing in hazy, polluted air

There are two cardboard boxes in Meenu Rana's living room in Delhi. One is filled with Diwali decorations and twinkling lights. The other holds air purifiers.

"The mornings have already started feeling stuffy. It will get worse in the coming days, so it's better to be prepared," says the 43-year-old teacher.

She is not alone. Every year, as winter and the festive season approach, residents of India's capital begin preparing for something far less celebratory: months of breathing polluted air.

Delhi and its satellite cities record some of the world's highest pollution levels through the year. But as winter sets in, the air often becomes unbearable.

Falling temperatures and slower winds trap pollutants close to the ground instead of allowing them to disperse. Smoke from crop residue burning in neighbouring states adds to emissions from vehicles and factories, as well as construction dust. Firecrackers used during festivals often worsen air quality.

The result is noxious air and a familiar question: why does pollution return year after year, and why hasn't more been done to tackle it?

This year, however, the Delhi government announced its Winter Action Plan months before the worst of the smog arrived. The plan is in place from 1 November to 28 February, covering the months when pollution usually spikes.

Experts have called this a step in the right direction, noting that in previous years measures were often rolled out only after the air quality got too bad to ignore. However, they say this by itself won't be enough, calling for more holistic, long-lasting measures.

Just days after the plan came into effect, the city is slipping back into a depressingly familiar pattern.

This week, a grey haze hangs over Delhi, along with a distinct smoky smell that residents unwillingly recognise. People complain of irritation in their eyes and difficulty breathing, and coughs and sneezes are heard everywhere.

For those who can afford them, air purifiers have become a household necessity. Outdoors, people wear masks or cover their faces with cloth, trying to protect themselves from the worst of the pollution.

Anti-smog guns sprinkle water to settle atmospheric dust in Delhi

Tackling pollution was among the promises made by the governing Bharatiya Janata Party during its election campaign last year. Its winter action plan is now a major test of that pledge.

The plan restricts the entry of older, more polluting vehicles registered outside Delhi and denies fuel to vehicles without a certificate which shows they meet emission standards. Demolitions and outdoor construction work that generate dust have been temporarily banned.

Private vehicle use is being discouraged - the government has asked offices to allow half of their employees to work from home, introduced staggered office timings for its staff and doubled parking charges. It says it will also strengthen pollution monitoring and enforcement and promote public transport and electric vehicles.

These measures are separate from the Graded Response Action Plan (Grap), which is overseen by the federal Commission for Air Quality Management.

Grap introduces restrictions in stages, with stricter measures kicking in as pollution levels rise. This week, its second stage is in force, restricting the use of diesel generators and burning of coal and firewood.

Expert say on paper, these actions collectively provide a roadmap for responding to rising pollution levels but their success ultimately depends on enforcement.

"There are a lot of positive changes as far as planning is concerned, but when it comes to execution, we probably still stand at the same level," says Parthaa Bosu, strategic adviser at the Environmental Defense Fund, a non-profit group focused on delivering climate solutions.

Others argue that the problem goes much deeper.

Vehicle entry restrictions have led to huge jams at Delhi's borders

"If we want to reduce pollution, we need to set targets for every sector - including power generation, industry and transport - not just vehicle entry, construction and stubble burning," says Manoj Kumar, an analyst at the Centre for Research on Energy and Clean Air.

"We know how much each sector and each state contributes to pollution. We can set targets to reduce emissions from each of them over the next five years."

Bosu points out that an issue which does not get enough attention is secondary pollutants - when gases in the air react with sunlight, water vapour and other substances to form harmful particles that contribute to pollution.

"Pollution is not just caused by biomass burning or vehicles," he says. "Dust being stirred up from the ground, coal and fly ash, materials burned at brick kilns and the large-scale burning of urban waste are also contributing to the problem. And much of this is still not being stopped."

Delhi's pollution problem is also affected by what happens outside the city. Pollutants travel into Delhi from neighbouring states and other parts of northern India, worsening the situation, Bosu explains.

This means that measures taken by Delhi can achieve only so much. Tackling the problem requires neighbouring states and the wider northern region to work together, he adds.

'I panic every time she coughs' - Delhi's toxic air is making its children sick

Why India caps pollution reading at 500 when the air is far more toxic

Can artificial rain fix toxic air in India's capital?

Living in Delhi smog is like watching a dystopian film again and again

In the meantime, pollution is harming people's health every day.

During winter, government monitoring stations in Delhi frequently record Air Quality Index (AQI) readings above 300, classified as "very poor".

On particularly bad days, readings can reach 500, the highest point on India's scale. That does not mean pollution cannot rise further, only that domestic trackers do not record it. International trackers such as Swiss-based IQAir have recorded readings close to 1,000 on some days.

The World Health Organization recommends average PM2.5 levels over 24 hours should not exceed 15 micrograms per cubic metre. In Delhi, levels are often at more than 25 times that limit during pollution season.

Dr Manoj Goel, pulmonology unit head at Fortis Memorial Research Institute near Delhi, says these tiny particles "can penetrate deep into the lungs and enter the bloodstream, triggering inflammation and affecting multiple organs".

Even short-term exposure can cause throat irritation, coughing, wheezing and breathlessness. It can also worsen existing heart and lung conditions.

A 2025 report, external by the Energy Policy Institute at the University of Chicago estimated that air pollution could reduce life expectancy in Delhi by up to 8.2 years.

Burning of crop residue in neighbouring states is among the contributors to Delhi's pollution

For residents, the challenge is not just limited to breathing. The restrictions also disrupt everyday life.

Delhi is closely connected to its satellite towns, Noida and Gurugram, with thousands of people travelling between them every day for work. Long queues form at the city's borders as vehicles are stopped and checked, leaving commuters stuck in traffic for hours.

While the new rules offer some hope, frustration is growing.

Protests, political arguments and court interventions have become part of the annual cycle. But despite public pressure and government measures, the pollution returns every winter.

Rana keeps her windows closed and curtains drawn, and has added more indoor plants.

The arrival of another winter fills her family with dread, but unlike many others, they are not planning to move out of Delhi.

"I would have left the city long ago had there been an option, but my son has his school, my husband and I have our jobs as well, which makes it practically impossible," she says.

"What option do we have? It is unfair but we will have to live with it."

#74. Beau Lamarre-Condon trial: prosecution begins cross-examination of double murder accused – as it happened

Beau Lamarre-Condon tells double murder trial shooting of Jesse Baird wasn’t intentional but ‘it was my fault’

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The court has adjourned for the day. Here are some of the key moments from Lamarre-Condon’s evidence today.

The former officer told the court about the day of the alleged murders of Jesse Baird and his partner, Luke Davies, on 19 February 2024 at Baird’s Paddington home. He claimed he disguised himself as an UberEats rider to try to avoid interacting with Jesse Baird.

The jury heard he entered the house to search for his “expensive” belongings including a Cartier ring.

Lamarre-Condon said he saw Baird with tape in one hand and a knife in the other, sealing boxes. This was when he alleges Baird lunged at the accused with the knife, the court heard, which led to a struggle between the two.

The former officer said he aimed to shoot Baird in the right shoulder to get him to drop the knife, saying he stands by the decision to take that shot but “never had the thought or intention” of killing Baird.

He told the court Luke Davies picked up his gun and pointed it at him before a second struggle ensued. Lamarre-Condon claims Davies shot himself.

Lamarre-Condon said he spoke with some friends about shooting Baird and disposing of the bodies.

The officer said he was “fearful” about going to police because he thought he would be killed in jail.

Lamarre-Condon said he is guilty of Baird’s manslaughter and turned himself into police on 27 February 2024 because he “needed to do the right thing”.

That’s where we’ll leave our coverage for today. The trial will continue at 10am on Monday.

The trial has adjourned for the day and will resume sitting on Monday.

#71. Bondi beach shooting: Eva Geller, 90, shot during massacre dies in hospital year after antisemitic attack

Eva Geller was shot on 14 December 2025 during Hanukah festival and she has been in and out of hospital since

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

A woman who was shot during the Bondi beach terror attack has died in hospital almost 12 months after the massacre.

Eva Geller, 90, had been in and out of hospital this year for treatment of injuries sustained during the attack on 14 December, a senior Jewish community source said on Friday.

She had recently returned to hospital after developing an infection, where she died after having a stroke, the source said.

In a statement on Friday afternoon, New South Wales police said they were aware of the death. A spokesperson said police were “awaiting further advice and are working with the office of the coroner”.

The prime minister, Anthony Albanese, said on Friday he was saddened to hear of Geller’s death, saying “her life was changed forever by an act of terror and antisemitism”.

“We are wrapping our arms around Eva’s family, friends and the Jewish community at this very sad time.”

The New South Wales premier, Chris Minns, said the news of Geller’s death was “heartbreaking”.

“My thoughts are with Eva’s family and the Jewish community as they face another painful loss,” he said in a statement.

The NSW opposition leader, Kellie Sloane, whose Vaucluse electorate takes in Bondi beach, said the death was “a heartbreaking reminder of the ongoing pain that remains for many people from last year’s terror attack”.

“We must continue to support the Jewish community, first responders and all victims.”

Fifteen people died after Naveed Akram, 24, and his father, 50-year-old Sajid Akram, opened fire at the Hanukkah festival at Bondi beach on 14 December last year. Forty people were injured.

Sajid was shot dead by police at the scene. Naveed has been charged with 78 offences, including 15 counts of murder and one count of committing a terrorist act that investigators allege may have been “inspired by Isis”. He remains before the courts.
