#66. Australia faces same issues that flipped control of US Congress in midterms, experts say

Anthony Albanese’s government has taken a blow due to Iran war but voter anxiety around cost of living and datacentres may hit harder

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

The Democrats’ stunning midterm victory should broaden Australia’s alliance with the US, observers say, even as a new power base emerges in Washington, affecting Aukus, datacentre development and relations with China.

Cabinet ministers, diplomats and Australia’s defence industrial sector closely watched the results this week, with control of the US Congress flipping to the Democrats and the outlook for Donald Trump’s final two years in office badly dinted.

Public fury over rising living costs, particularly fuel prices, and the impact of datacentres, were also two factors in the midterms results that carry warnings for the Australian government, analysts said.

Bruce Wolpe, a senior fellow at the United States Studies Centre, said Trump’s ability to rile up voters had been undermined by cost of living.

Wolpe said the Albanese government was taking a hit because of the economic fallout from the Iran war.

“I think a lot of people are wondering what does this [Australian] government do?” he said. “Have they lost control of the economy?

“In January, before the Iran war, the outlook looked pretty good. But now, after going through a budget process and the war … people are just very uncertain as to where this is going to end up.

“It is not an immediately solvable problem, and people really dislike it.”

Westpac’s head of international economics, Elliot Clarke, said the landslide midterms results had “dramatically changed” the outlook for the Iran war, which could affect the economy in Australia.

“It has changed the ‘atmospherics’ around the Middle East conflict,” he said. “It’s positive news.”

There was a steep drop in the global oil price as the election results filtered out. The Brent crude benchmark, which had threatened to pass its April peak, has instead plunged by 20%.

More confidence in a potential resolution in the Middle East would also help curb inflation and help central banks hold back on rate hikes, according to Clarke.

“It definitely changes the calculus,” he said.

Coalition frontbencher Andrew Hastie said the results showed economic messaging was critical.

“People feel worse off after tariffs and after the war in Iran, and Donald Trump has said a few times now that he doesn’t really care,” he told 2GB. “Our job is always to deliver better outcomes for the Australian people.”

Arthur Sinodinos, a former ambassador to Washington, said the change on Capitol Hill was a chance for Australian officials to progress policy goals with both US parties, and to consider the relationship beyond the 2028 presidential election.

“The main question mark is: ‘What is the impact on geopolitics across the world?’” he said. “Does this inject more uncertainty into American foreign policy? We’ve already had a lot of volatility in that regard.”

“The Congress may put more pressure on the administration to support Ukraine and pass measures that involve funding munitions to Ukraine.

“I think the Chinese will feel more emboldened, but I don’t think that translates into some sudden action on Taiwan. Trump has made it clear in the past that they won’t do anything on his watch, and I think he would feel bound to act if they did.”

Sinodinos praised prime minister Anthony Albanese’s professional approach to dealing with the often unconventional US president.

“I think the Australian government’s reaction after this vote is keep working with the administration, keep their heads down,” he said.

Trump claimed that Republicans had fared poorly because he was not on the ballot, despite repeatedly urging voters to pretend he was on the ballot. The vice-president, JD Vance, called Trump’s war in Iran “the obvious culprit”.

Ed Husic, former industry minister, wants a radical shake up in the alliance.

“The moment to reset the relationship with the Trump administration is right now,” he said.

“A completely different Congress will require that administration to do things differently, including not hurting allies – friends, such as Australia – with tariffs.”

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

Abe Denmark, a partner at The Asia Group and an architect of the Aukus agreement in the Biden administration, warned against over-interpreting the results for Australia.

“What will change is how power is distributed in Washington,” he said.

“There will now be new centres of power to engage: Democratic chairs controlling authorisations and appropriations, Republicans more willing to break ranks, and 2028 hopefuls from both parties looking to stand out.

“While this gives Australia more points to engage, it also generates more noise. The challenge for Canberra, as with all allies and partners, will be to differentiate political positioning from durable policy.”

Denmark said the Democrats could take a different view from Trump on Aukus spending, as the US submarine industrial base needs a dramatic step up in production.

He said a government funding deal and passage of a key defence spending bill were urgent priorities for Congress.

Husic also flagged possible changes to the US’s approach to AI regulation, and a flow on effect as the Albanese government prepare major legislation for 2027. Vermont senator Bernie Sanders has already called for a moratorium on new construction, warning that voters wanted the environment and humanity itself protected.

Greens senator Sarah Hanson-Young said it was no coincidence that datacentre hotspots like Texas, Georgia and Ohio had shifted to support Democrats.

“Communities are being asked to hand over their land, water and power to some of the world’s wealthiest tech companies, yet the benefits for ordinary Australians remain remarkable difficult to identify,” she said.

The acting prime minister, Richard Marles, observed cost-of-living and datacentre anxiety was at play in both countries.

“What we are very mindful about here is that you’ve got to get the social licence right in respect of datacentres – where they’re built, and the place where they shouldn’t be built is in suburbs – and also making sure that the right power and the right water is brought to these datacentres,” he said.

#61. Beau Lamarre-Condon tells double murder trial shooting of Jesse Baird wasn’t intentional but ‘it was my fault’

Former NSW police officer gives evidence for a second day after pleading not guilty to murdering Baird and Luke Davies in early 2024

Beau Lamarre-Condon trial live updates: former police officer continues evidence in double murder trial

Beau Lamarre-Condon “stands by” his decision to shoot Jesse Baird but did not intend to kill him, the New South Wales supreme court has heard.

The former police officer is accused of fatally shooting Baird and his partner, Luke Davies, with his police service weapon in Baird’s Paddington home in Sydney on 19 February 2024.

He has pleaded not guilty to two counts of murder and one charge of aggravated break and enter.

Lamarre-Condon continued giving evidence in the double murder trial on Friday, telling the court about the moment he allegedly shot Baird.

“That first shot into Jesse’s right shoulder was the right move. I stand by it,” he said.

He claimed Baird had lunged at him with a knife and told the court he deliberately aimed his gun at the right shoulder, believing it would not kill Baird.

“As police officers we are trained to shoot people at the centre of body mass … I had a split-second moment where I said, ‘Drop the knife’ … It was just police mode now,” he said from the witness box.

“If I didn’t do that I was 110% going to have that knife driven into my throat.”

However, Lamarre-Condon repeatedly told the court he “did not want to kill Jesse”.

“Jesse did not deserve anything like that to happen to him,” he told the court.

“He did nothing to me to deserve to be killed.”

During his second day in the witness box, Lamarre-Condon told the jury he entered Baird’s home on 19 February 2024 because he wanted to retrieve his belongings from the house without being recognised.

He claimed he did not want to come into contact with Baird due to a risk the other man could take out an apprehended violence order against him as Baird had previously asked him to “please refrain from contacting me”.

Lamarre-Condon told the court he had seen what he assumed to be someone’s delivery motorcycle: “As soon as I saw that I had an idea … I saw a hi-vis hooded jacket, waterproof long pants and gloves.

“This moment here … I wish that I didn’t do this … But I had the thought process that if I was to put on this helmet and outfit I could knock on Jesse’s door I would look like an UberEats driver without any verbal interaction with Jesse,” he said.

The accused thought Baird’s room was empty when he entered the home, he told the court.

He searched through some storage boxes in Baird’s home in search of his belongings, including a Cartier ring and a $2,000 shirt, but could not find them, he said.

When he looked up, he saw Baird with a knife and tape sealing boxes and said the other man began yelling, “we’re getting robbed”, the jury heard.

Lamarre-Condon said he was still wearing the motorcycle helmet and his firearm in a holster under his shirt when Baird saw him.

“He started yelling, ‘We’re getting robbed, we’re getting robbed’ … He couldn’t see it was me because the visor was down,” Lamarre-Condon said.

Lamarre-Condon told the court Baird pinned him against a wall with his left forearm while “swinging around the knife” in his other hand as the officer yelled “It’s Beau, calm the fuck down”.

He said he then had a “very, very clear thought”.

“[I thought] shoot him in the shoulder so you don’t kill him, so he drops the knife,” he told the court.

Lamarre-Condon claimed he then fired the gun, but the two continued to struggle and the officer’s finger remained on the trigger, the jury heard.

They tripped over a storage box on the ground, which is when Lamarre-Condon then “accidentally” discharged the gun.

He told the court: “It was my fault … I am guilty of manslaughter … But it wasn’t intentional.”

Under cross-examination from crown prosecutor Brett Hatfield SC, Lamarre-Condon repeated that he “stands by” the decision to take the first shot.

“If I didn’t, Mr Crown, you would be sitting here today interrogating Mr Baird,” he said.

Asked by Hatfield if he shot Baird in the back, Lamarre-Condon said: “that’s not accurate, that’s not true”.

While Lamarre-Condon said Baird’s shooting was his fault, he maintained Davies shot himself.

After the first two shots, the jury heard Lamarre-Condon dialled the emergency hotline to try to get help for Baird.

But when he looked up, he saw Davies, who he didn’t recognise at the time.

The accused claimed the other man had picked up his gun.

“It was pointed towards me at my chest and he said ‘I’m going to fucking shoot you,’” he told the court.

“I started lowering my body towards the ground: I said, ‘Please don’t shoot me, I’m not here to shoot you. I’m not a threat I’m not here to hurt you.’

“That’s when he raised the gun towards my head and said, ‘Fuck off.’”

The former officer said police are trained to redirect gun muzzles when firearms are pointed at them.

“I saw the gun, he was holding it with both hands I thought fuck he’s going to fire the gun and that’s where my police training kicked in,” he told the court.

“My left hand would have grabbed Luke’s left wrist and turned it away … my other hand pushed against the forearm and then I pushed the pivot point of the left wrist away from me.”

Asked by the defence lawyer whether the gun went off, Lamarre-Condon said: “Yes, during this motion. And it got Luke.”

Baird’s mother, Helen, left the courtroom partway through Lamarre-Condon’s evidence.

The trial before Justice Natalie Adams continues.

#56. South Korea says it will expand naval operations closer to strait of Hormuz

Seoul to seek parliamentary approval to extend anti-piracy Cheonghae unit’s area to about 650km from the vital strait after criticism from Donald Trump on Iran war support

South Korea plans to expand the operations ⁠of its anti-piracy unit to waters closer to the strait of Hormuz, its defence ministry has said, after pressure from Donald Trump to ⁠provide support for his war in Iran.

The government is to submit a motion to parliament that would expand the Cheonghae unit’s area of operation to waters near the port of Duqm in Oman, which ​is about 650km (403 miles) away from the strait. The motion also needs to be approved by ‌President Lee Jae Myung and the cabinet.

“The enhancement of the Cheonghae unit’s mission is to protect our citizens and safeguard our economic interests,” a defence ministry official told reporters.

The reinforcement was strictly for non-combat purposes and the unit would operate independently of allied forces, said the official, speaking on condition of anonymity.

“Operations will remain restricted to the open waters south of Oman, and the maritime patrol aircraft will be prohibited from targeting or monitoring Iranian territory, territorial waters or the interior of the strait of Hormuz during their missions,” he said.

The move came as Iran’s Revolutionary ⁠Guard (IRGC) said ⁠two ⁠oil tankers ​and a tugboat had caught ⁠fire in separate incidents after attempting ⁠to use unauthorised routes ​through ‌the strait.

One tanker ‌struck a mine while attempting to cross a passage south of the waterway, ‌while another supertanker caught fire while exiting ​the route in the eastern part of the strait, ⁠the IRGC said on Thursday. A ​tugboat ​that approached ​the second vessel ​to ‌help extinguish the ​fire ​also caught fire.

Trump has criticised South Korea for what he called insufficient support for the Iran war and scaled back major joint ⁠military drills by the two countries’ armed forces this summer, a move that ​unsettled the US ally.

The unit has been stationed in the Gulf of Aden since 2009 to ensure safe navigation of commercial vessels.

The motion also seeks to ​extend the unit’s deployment to the Middle ‌East, which is set ‌to expire at the end of 2026, a ministry spokesperson said, confirming a report by Yonhap ‌news agency.

It currently includes a 4,400-ton-class destroyer, a maritime operations helicopter, up to three rigid-hull inflatable boats and about 300 service members, according to broadcaster YTN.

Seoul would also deploy a P-8A maritime patrol aircraft and increase the unit’s personnel by 75, the Korea Herald reported.

South ​Korea relies heavily on oil ​imports and sourced 70% of its supplies from the Middle East in ​2025, most of ‌it delivered through the ​strait of Hormuz, ​according to the industry ministry.

Lee said on 18 September that he would not deploy military assets to the Middle East in a way that could draw the country into conflict, but that Seoul was reviewing playing a greater role in safeguarding navigation in the region.

#51. UK found to be ‘dangerously’ reliant on China for critical minerals needed for defence and AI

Delivering net zero also at risk, say MPs as they call for centralised policy and funding for mines and diversification

The UK is “dangerously” dependent on China for critical raw materials needed for the military, renewable energy infrastructure and AI, a parliamentary investigation has found.

MPs on the business select committee called for an urgent centralised policy and the creation of a “critical minerals finance facility” to help fund rare earth and other critical mineral mines, and deploy grants, equity and debt to accelerate a diversification programme.

“Critical minerals are the alpha of the new economy,” said the committee’s chair, Liam Byrne. “We cannot rearm Britain, build AI or deliver net zero unless we secure the minerals these technologies depend on.

“Yet 16 years after China first weaponised its chokehold over rare earths, Britain remains dangerously exposed.”

It is the second time in a week that the government’s policy on China has been criticised by Byrne’s committee.

China has a stranglehold on the world’s supply of rare earths and other critical minerals, as well as their refined byproducts that are used in everything from military jets to car-locking systems and consumer products such as fridges and washing machines.

The committee heard that “the processing stage” was where the bottlenecks were, with China dominating the refining of seven categories of minerals, including refined lithium needed for electric vehicles and rare earths used in the fuselage and wings of military aircraft to help withstand temperature extremes.

MPs were told that demand for lithium alone would increase 45-fold between now and 2030, leaving the UK in a high-risk dependency relationship with China.

The demand for rare earths needed for permanent magnets used in military jets and other applications would rise 16-fold in the same period as Britain was seeking to rebuild its military capabilities.

But with China capable of suddenly turning off rare earth supplies, as it did in 2025, a dependency on Beijing was a strategic vulnerability for the UK economy and national security, the committee found.

Separate restrictions on legacy chips after a row over a Dutch intervention in the China-owned Nexperia left the European car industry “days away” from stoppages last October.

The EU, the US and other countries faced the same risk of supplies of rare earths being cut off by China, and the committee said the UK was not putting enough effort into finding alternative supplies.

Donald Trump secured a one-year reprieve on global export restrictions on rare earths from Xi Jinping in October last year. This was extended to January 2027 after the Chinese president’s recent visit to Washington.

However, the reprieve means the UK is at the mercy of US-China relations, the report suggests.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

The most “realistic” solution was for the UK to work closely with G7 partners, the rest of Europe and Indo-Pacific countries, to “aggregate demand” and bring in new suppliers.

The committee said its investigation was frustrated by lack of transparency by the government.

“Parliament has no visibility of the capabilities the government is seeking to protect, the vulnerabilities it has identified, or the projects it is backing to reduce risk,” it said.

The committee has drawn up a 10-point plan that includes: working to identify domestic mining; refining and recycling opportunities and their costs; a strategy to encourage business to reduce procurement from China; a plan to unlock investment; and assess planning and permitting processes that may prove a barrier to mining.

As it stood, the committee said it could not be confident the government could satisfy any of its 10 tests.

“The UK requires new institutional and financial muscle,” it concluded.

A government spokesperson denied it did not have a critical minerals strategy saying it was delivering £400m “to ramp up domestic production and recycling” and forge new international partnerships. “Our strategy also sets clear goals for 2035, including meeting 10% of demand through domestic production and 20% through recycling, with no more than 60% of any critical minerals coming from any single country,” they added.

#48. Melania Trump calls for lasting Ukraine truce as she hosts Russian and Ukrainian officials

Watch: US First Lady Melania Trump given Ukraine's highest award for women

US First Lady Melania Trump called for a "more enduring" ceasefire in Ukraine as she welcomed Russian and Ukrainian delegations at the White House following last week's truce in the southern region of Kherson.

Hundreds of residents of the Russian-occupied Ukrainian town of Oleshky facing starvation and constant bombardment were able to evacuate during a six-day ceasefire, which both Moscow and Kyiv credited Trump with brokering.

"The lives of innocent people must transcend the division of war. I hope Oleshky inspires greater peace," she said.

The unusual White House event saw senior Ukrainian and Russian officials meet away from the setting of official US-brokered peace talks they have been holding without success since last year.

The delegations were made up of Ukraine's human rights ombudsman Dmytro Lubinets and his Russian counterpart Yana Lantratova, as well as Ukrainian MP Davyd Arakhamia and Kremlin envoy Kirill Dmitriev.

Melania Trump was credited by all for playing a key though unspecified role in the ceasefire, and all parties conveyed to her the gratitude of the Russian and Ukrainian presidents.

Melania Trump handed Dmitriev and Arakhamia "personal letters" for their respective presidents

The US first lady said she had "reviewed demining plans, aerial images, assessed roadways, and discussed evacuation routes, hospital access, vehicles, and medical resources" during the truce.

"Last week in Oleshky and neighbouring areas of Kherson, people experienced the simple joys of life for the first time since 2022," Trump said. "They walked down familiar streets without fear of explosions."

She thanked both Volodymyr Zelensky and Vladimir Putin, making no reference to the origins of the war, which began when the Russian president launched a full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, and which has caused thousands of civilian casualties and the displacement of millions.

Dmitriev, a businessman and Kremlin special envoy who has previously represented Moscow during peace talks with Ukraine, thanked Trump for "saving and touching so many lives".

"Let us continue making a humanitarian contribution together; this is within our power," Dmitriev said, before gifting Melania Trump a framed drawing by an 11-year-old Oleshky evacuee.

He also praised Trump for her work on reuniting children separated from their families by the war. These include children allegedly abducted by Russia.

Arakhamia, a Ukrainian MP who heads Zelensky's party in parliament who has also been part of the Ukrainian negotiating team, said the Oleshky ceasefire had constituted the "biggest de-escalation since 2022".

"We are creating a momentum where both sides are learning theâ¦ mechanics of how to actually keep the peace when the peace comes," he added.

He acknowledged that the "political track" of negotiations to end the conflict was "complex" but said Ukrainians were asking Melania Trump "to keep going and find new missions and de-escalate more."

At the end of the event, Trump handed Dmitriev and Arakhamia two letters to be hand-delivered to their respective presidents.

The White House later said Melania Trump had invited her guests to view two historic artifacts which she had "personally selected as powerful symbols of peace".

One was an 18th Century medicine chest which was removed from the White House when the British set it on fire in 1812. "Once adversaries, the United States and Great Britain ultimately established a strong alliance," the White House said.

The second artifact was a cup gifted in 1899 to the French ambassador in recognition of his efforts to bring the Spanish-American war to an end.

Melania Trump has generally remained discreet on foreign policy matters, and neither Moscow nor Kyiv have elaborated on the nature or extent of her involvement in coordinating the delicate ceasefire - the most enduring and widespread of the war so far.

A source who works in the local government of the Kherson region told the BBC that work on the Oleshky evacuation had been ongoing for almost a year.

Oleshky evacuees arrived in Ukraine after a journey through southern Russia and Belarus

However, the source said, "at a certain point other individuals became involved, seemingly after most of the groundwork had already been laid and subsequently began taking credit for the progress that had been made."

It was difficult to determine exactly who had played what role and at which stage of the process, the source acknowledged, adding that "those who had been directly involved in the negotiations for nearly a year were eventually prohibited from communicating publicly about the matter."

Speaking after the meeting in the White House had taken place, President Zelensky said the success of the Oleshky ceasefire "had proved a very simple thing: when Putin wants the fighting to stop, it stops."

"The same applies to our people who are held in Russian captivity. When Putin wants, they go home."

A source close to American officials shared a similar assessment, implying to the BBC that despite the outward praise, "the person who made [the ceasefire] possible, whether we like it or not, is essentially Putin."

"And this was done in order to pass the ball to [Donald] Trump... so that it would have some positive effect for his midterm elections."

There has been no change whatsoever in Russia's position, Ukrainian political analyst Dmytro Levus told the BBC.

Moscow "agreed to it because there was pressure from the American side... In doing so, the Russians confirmed Zelenskyy's words that, when necessary, it turns out Russia can quickly stop firing and even maintain that ceasefire."

As the frontline region of Kherson experienced its first six days of peace in several years, the rest of Ukraine continued to come under Russian attack.

This week, several drone and missile attacks killed and injured civilians across the country. On Monday alone, more than 40 people were killed in a single Russian strike on an aid distribution centre in the region of Zaporizhzhia. More are thought to still be buried under the rubble.

Additional reporting by Anastasiia Levchenko in Kyiv

#45. France needs 'more public services', 'salaries are too low', 'schools are in very, very bad shape'

As France confronts another wave of strikes and protests, Gaëlle Martinez, National Secretary of the trade union Solidaires, presents the general strike as more than a confrontation over wages, public spending or the cost of living. #44. Who could be behind hoax bomb threats to European air travel?

Authorities will be scrambling to follow any information that might lead them to a culprit

The warnings came in thick and fast, targeting one airline or airport after another.

At least 21 flights and several airports across Europe were affected by bomb threats on Thursday.

In nearly all cases - London's Heathrow airport being a notable exception - they were taken to be credible enough to divert the aircraft in question, or even in Italy's case, to scramble fighter jets to escort it.

They all turned out to be false, which does not, of course, mean that the next ones will not be real.

It is hardly surprising that the simultaneous, co-ordinated nature of these airline hoaxes, coupled with other malicious actions across Europe, are causing some people to point the finger of suspicion at Russia.

Following a series of cyber-attacks and mysterious drone sightings over sensitive sites earlier this year, in the past few days we have seen the remote hacking of supermarket freezers, as well as a blood bank, a suspicious incident on Poland's border with Russia, and in August a drone attack Germany's Leipzig Halle airport which its interior minister publicly blamed on Moscow.

There are many who argue, based purely on circumstantial evidence, that this must surely be yet another example of "hybrid", "grey zone" or "sub-threshold" warfare - actions that damage a nation's infrastructure or society but which tend to be anonymous, deniable and which fall short of triggering Nato's Article 5 and thereby mobilising a response by the West.

Moscow is certainly furious with Nato for supporting Ukraine and, in doing so, frustrating the Kremlin's plans to swallow up that country and effectively terminate its independence.

Russian bloggers have also suggested that, if Ukraine's drones are currently making Russia's airports unsafe, and Western sanctions are blocking Russian airlines from European airspace, then why shouldn't Western nations feel the same pain?

But as of Thursday evening no hard evidence had emerged linking the airline hoaxes to Russia, Iran or any proxy groups controlled by them.

There was the email threat sent on Thursday morning to airport authorities in Rome, purporting to be from "Fighters of the Male State".

That name likely refers to the Russian group Muzhskoi Legion or "Male Legion", an ultra-right group with such extreme views it was banned by a Moscow court in 2021.

Yet, it retained tens of thousands of online followers on Telegram as it cheered on the full-scale Russian invasion of Ukraine in the following year.

But the email sent to Rome, even if genuine - and who is to say it is not another group hiding behind that name? - is unlikely to provide the leads that police and airport authorities need to pin down who is generating these hoaxes and why.

For national police forces, airport authorities and transport ministries trying to work out who is behind these hoaxes and how best to mitigate their effect when they happen again, the task is not to go with who they think might be the most likely culprits but instead to follow the cold, hard leads - if they can find them.

Hoax bomb threats target European airlines and airports for second time in a week

#43. Hong Kong pro-democracy activist Joshua Wong sentenced to 10 years in prison in national security case

Wong, 30, in prison since 2020, is only activist in Hong Kong prosecuted twice under national security law

Exclusive: Joshua Wong laments city’s fate from inside maximum security prison

The Hong Kong activist Joshua Wong has been sentenced to 10 years in prison after earlier pleading guilty to “foreign collusion”, his second conviction under a national security law critics say has been used to silence political opposition in the city.

Wong, who rose to prominence as the teenage face of Hong Kong’s 2014 pro-democracy demonstrations, submitted his guilty plea in September. He had been accused of working with the exiled Hong Kong activist Nathan Law to urge foreign powers to impose sanctions, blockades or other hostile measures against China.

In his ruling on Thursday, the high ​court judge William Tam ‌described Wong’s actions as “grave in nature”.

He said Wong had visited the US to seek foreign interference before the national security law’s introduction in 2020, and continued to share posts on social media by Law calling for sanctions after it was enacted.

Wong’s role was to “supply his underground support” and “arouse the attention of foreign countries to bolster the cause”, the judge said. He described Wong’s actions as part of a “scheme” to collude with foreign governments.

Tam said Wong’s sentence would run concurrently with the remaining term of his previous national security offence.

After the ruling, Law said in a statement: “It’s incredibly sad to see my friend Joshua Wong suffer another sentencing over his peaceful advocacy work for Hong Kong’s democracy. The signal the sentencing sent to the world is that [China] is determined to quash any hopes for freedom and democracy on its soil.”

Amnesty International’s deputy regional director, Sarah Brooks, said: “Today’s sentence is another grim reminder of how Hong Kong’s national security law has become a tool to punish dissent rather than to genuinely protect national security.”

Wong wore a dark blue shirt and thick rimmed spectacles in court, and nodded and bowed as supporters waved. Dozens of people had waited in line outside the court building for the hearing, amid a heavy police presence. A group of international diplomats was also present for the sentencing.

The former student leader is already serving a prison term of four years and eight months, imposed in November 2024, for conspiracy to commit subversion for his role in an unofficial primary election. He has spent nearly six years in detention and had been due to be released in January 2027.

The US consulate in Hong Kong said in a statement that it “strongly condemns” the additional sentence and called for Wong’s immediate release. “Wong has been unjustly imprisoned since December 2020, demonstrating that Beijing will go to extraordinary lengths to silence those who advocate for fundamental freedoms in Hong Kong,” a consular spokesperson said.

Shortly afterwards, China warned against “irresponsible” remarks on Hong Kong’s judicial affairs. “Relevant parties should earnestly respect China’s sovereignty, respect Hong Kong’s legal system, stop making irresponsible remarks regarding the trial of … judicial cases,” the foreign ministry spokesperson, Guo Jiakun, told reporters.

Wong, 30, gained international attention during the 2014 Umbrella protests, which paralysed the city’s financial centre and galvanised a generation of young people. In October 2014, he appeared on the cover of Time magazine under the headline: “The face of protest.”

When protests erupted in 2019 over a proposed extradition bill, Wong was serving a prison sentence for his role in the 2014 demonstrations. He was released at the height of the protests and immediately took to the streets.

The following year, Beijing introduced a sweeping national security law it said was needed to restore stability to the former British colony, but critics say has been used to target political opposition, dissolve civil society groups and silence independent media.

Wong is the only activist in Hong Kong prosecuted twice under the law.

#40. Eight killed in series of shootings in western Turkey

The shootings took place in rural areas northeast of the city of Afyonkarahisar (pictured)

Eight people have been killed and one seriously injured in a series of shootings in western Turkey.

The attacks took place in the Afyonkarahisar province on Thursday, with victims found in several locations in three separate vehicles, according to local reports.

The suspect fled, triggering a manhunt, and authorities later said he had been arrested in EskiÅehir province.

CCTV footage published in Turkish media showed a man in a black jacket, white trousers and glasses clutching a long weapon as he stood by a red minivan.

Turkey's state-run Anadolu Agency reported that three people were killed near Ayazini village and three others near Kiyir village. In Yukaritandir, two women were found injured and later died in hospital.

Two teachers were reportedly among the dead.

Local media said roads leading to the affected villages were closed by police, while officers launched a large-scale operation to find the suspect.

The newspaper Milliyet reported that a vehicle allegedly taken by the attacker had been later found stuck in mud - the suspect having abandoned it before fleeing on foot.

In a statement posted on social media, the Afyonkarahisar Governorate said "all relevant institutions of our state have been working in coordination since the very first moment and are meticulously carrying out the necessary work".

#38. K-pop group NewJeans’s contract omitted rights to ‘sleep and free choice’ – report

New report finds that record label Ador stripped child protections from group’s contracts when they were minors

The superstar K-pop group NewJeans signed restrictive record label contracts that waived their right to “sleep, rest and free choice”.

A new report from the New York Times found that the contracts were signed when the members of the record-breaking girl group were aged 14 to 17.

The contracts banned the performers from having an “insincere attitude” and engaging in “inappropriate romantic relationships”. They also struck standard child protection language which protected “basic human rights” including the group members’ right to education.

The contracts from record label Ador (a subsidiary of Hybe, home of the popular groups BTS and Katseye) additionally excluded portions of the Korean government’s K-pop contract template stipulating that the label “shall not demand that a performer who is a minor engage in excessive physical exposure or overly sexually suggestive expression”.

In a statement to the New York Times, Hybe representatives said the company adheres to child-protection laws and works hard to make sure its artists work and train in safe conditions.

NewJeans are one of the decade’s most popular K-pop acts. In 2023, the five-member group followed Blackpink to become the second Korean girl-group to score a number one album on the Billboard charts. In November 2024, the band made global headlines when they announced their decision to leave the label amid contract disputes and claims that Ador had deliberately undermined their careers.

Band member Hanni alleged that NewJeans were mistreated by the label and accused Ador of “deliberate miscommunications and manipulation” in a statement to South Korea’s parliament last year.

Ador has consistently maintained that they did not violate their duty. “A unilateral claim that trust has been broken does not constitute valid grounds for termination of a contract,” the label said in a statement.

The label sued band member Danielle, a member of her family and the band’s producer in December 2025 for a total of nearly $30m for damages and a contract break penalty.

Hybe representatives told the New York Times that its exit penalty fees do not exceed industry standards.

In July, the group promised that 2026 would be the “summer of NewJeans”, with a teaser showing the group as a four-piece sans Danielle. New music has yet to emerge.

The new reports expose the shadowy world of K-pop contracts and shine a light on the restrictive conditions that the genre’s biggest stars can be subject to.

By the time they signed their contracts, the NewJeans members – Minji, Hanni, Haerin, Hyein and Danielle – had already clocked in years of training in K-pop “boot camps” which taught vocal training, choreography and foreign language education.

Schedules were strict, one source told the New York Times, and it was not unusual for rehearsals to stretch as late as 2am. NewJeans’s youngest member, Hyein, was 12 at the time.

After the band members received their exclusive contracts with Ador in January 2022, the parents of one member requested multiple changes. They were told that if they did not sign the contract in its current form, “the contract itself” could be reconsidered.

In a statement to the New York Times, Hybe said its contracts were legal and negotiated in good faith, with protections in place for the artist as well as the label’s investment in them.

That contract struck government language that forbade performers working for “excessive periods of time”. It also stipulated that the band would have to repay a significant percentage of the plastic surgery costs that are common in K-pop.

“We do not even recommend cosmetic surgery or procedures against an individual’s wishes,” Hybe said in a statement.

The NewJeans contract also granted Ador rights to use the band members’ “name, photographs, likeness, handwriting, voice and any other elements” in perpetuity, in a level of ownership that goes further than government recommendations for such rights to end at the point of contract termination.

The clause also grants Ador the right to use the NewJeans members as AI chatbots or clones.

The Guardian has reached out to Hybe for comment.

#35. Democratic socialist defeats incumbent Karen Bass in Los Angeles mayor's race

Nithya Raman, once seen as a long-shot candidate, will be LA's new mayor

Democratic socialist Nithya Raman has toppled incumbent Karen Bass in the race for mayor of Los Angeles.

Raman's win stunned political watchers in California after she entered the race as a long-shot candidate against Bass, a political heavyweight who previously served in Congress and had never lost a race for political office.

Raman has frequently been compared to New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani. Both are democratic socialists and ran on progressive platforms.

Her win solidifies that America's two biggest cities will now be led by democratic socialists, which include some of the most high-profile politicians in the US, such as Mamdani and Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez.

Los Angeles was always likely to have a Democratic mayor but the question in the city - the second most populous city in the US - was just how left voters would be willing to go.

Bass, also Democrat, announced that she had called and conceded to Raman in a post on social media on Thursday, writing "while this is not the outcome we hoped for, the people of Los Angeles have spoken."

"Moving this city forward is what Los Angeles needs from all of us," Bass wrote. "I love Los Angeles. I believe in Los Angeles. And I will continue to ensure the promise of a better life belongs to all of us."

Food, fuel and taxes â can the Democrats actually make life cheaper?

'This is a disaster': Angry Republicans blame Trump for midterms defeat

Raman, a member of city council and Indian immigrant, ran on a platform of change and fixing the "broken status quo and the politicians who failed them over and over again".

She ran on cutting homelessness, increasing housing in the city, saving Hollywood jobs from continued cuts and protecting residents from immigration enforcement efforts. Raman also vowed to fix the city's many issues, including helping beautification efforts, fixing roads and even helping bring back nightlife in the city.

The major issue in the race, however, was the LA fires, which killed dozens of people and razed neighbourhoods across the area in 2025. Bass faced intense political fallout over her absence during the emergency and the city's response to the disaster.

The next mayor in LA will oversee hosting the Super Bowl in 2027 and the 2028 Summer Olympics.

Watch: Mamdani says he's a democratic socialist. What does that mean?

Raman entered the race as a long-shot candidate but slowly shored up support, especially from younger voters, before besting reality star Spencer Pratt in the primary race earlier this year.

Raman's win over Bass has all the appearances of echoing voter sentiment across the US as part of sweeping Democratic wins, including in deep-Republican country that hadn't seen liberal leaders in decades.

Washington DC also elected democratic socialist Janeese Lewis George as its new mayor, furthering progressive leadership in another major US city.

US President Donald Trump was asked at the White House about the wins by politicians on the left flank of the Democratic Party and said, "If you go by history, it's going to be a disaster".

Such candidates have become a focal point of attacks by Republicans.

Bass had been a prominent member of Congress before becoming LA's first black female mayor. Her roots are closely tied to LA and its history, even working as a community organiser during the violent 1992 riots over police brutality in the beating of Rodney King, who was black.

#34. Duchess of Sussex’s estranged half-brother ‘person of interest’ in child sexual abuse inquiry

Philippine official says there are ‘signs that point to Thomas Markle Jr’s involvement in crime’, but he’s not yet being treated as a suspect

The Duchess of Sussex’s estranged half-brother, Thomas Markle Jr, has been named as a “person of interest” in the Philippine authorities’ inquiry into child sexual abuse image allegations made against her father, Thomas Markle Sr.

The country’s interior and local government secretary, Jonvic Remulla, told a press conference in Manila there were “signs that point that the son is involved in the crime as well”.

He added that Markle Jr, 60, who alerted local police and the FBI to images he found in his father’s belongings, was not yet being treated as a suspect. His alleged involvement is not thought to be directly linked to the material he handed to the police.

More than 7,000 photos and one video – all of under-age males and females of Asian, Latin and European descent, with some as young as 10 and 11 – were uncovered, Remulla said.

He described the images as “the most deplorable displays of impunity towards sexual activity that I have ever seen”.

Markle Sr, 82, who is estranged from Meghan, has reportedly been moved into a care home for dementia patients in the US where he is now living.

Remulla said: “We’re determining if [the] son is involved … There are signs that point that the son is involved in the crime as well but only as a person of interest so far.”

He added: “We will know in the next few days.”

Markle Sr, who returned to the US from the Philippines in May, has admitted paying for sex in the Philippines but he has strongly denied possessing child abuse imagery, and suggested he had been “set up”. His son remains in Cebu City in the Philippines.

Remulla has previously described Markle Sr as “a possible sex offender of the grossest kind”.

Markle Jr reported his father after claiming to have found four storage boxes at his father’s home in the Philippines containing “hundreds, if not thousands” of child sex abuse images.

Police and FBI agents have since been investigating whether Markle Sr was in possession of any illegal images while living in the Philippines and in Mexico, where he previously lived, and where other illegal images were reportedly found in one of his storage units.

Markle Jr was recently interviewed at his home in Cordova, Cebu, as part of an international, multi-agency meeting via Microsoft Teams, according to the Sun, which first published the claims. He gave his statement to an FBI agent based in Florida, who specialises in child sex abuse cases.

In a statement issued when the allegations against her father were first published, Meghan said she was “deeply distressed” by the claims. She said she had been informed of disturbing allegations against her father in 2018 and had kept him from having contact with her children, Prince Archie, seven, and Princess Lilibet, five, to protect them.

#32. French protests see numbers surge as unions join strikes over high cost of living

The head of France's CGT labour union, Sophie Binet (C), said the wave of protests and strikes had reached a new level

More than 600,000 protesters have taken part in rallies and marches across France, according to France's interior ministry, with five unions joining a day of action demanding more money for wages and public services.

The left-wing CGT union put the number of people taking part in Thursday's mobilisation at 1.57 million and hailed the figure as "a level unmatched" since 2023, citing big protests in Paris, Marseille, Bordeaux and Lille.

As darkness fell in Paris, Prime Minister SÃ©bastien Lecornu said he would convene a "social conference" with unions and employers next Monday.

The wave of protests began in September over poor conditions and resources at lycÃ©es (high schools), before spreading to universities, workers and a revived "yellow-vest" movement against high prices.

The unions had vowed to take the co-ordinated move against the government "to the next level", and CGT chief Sophie Binet had said their message was that "the only thing that will bring this mobilisation to a halt is responding to our demands".

Interior Minister Laurent NuÃ±ez had earlier deployed 70,000 police, vowing "an extremely firm response" to any potential outbreaks of violence.

Some unrest was reported in several cities.

A car was set alight near the Gallieni bridge over the RhÃ´ne river in Lyon, as hundreds of activists from the radical left-wing "black bloc" clashed with police.

Clashes were also reported in the north-western city of Rennes, where the local prefect said a "hostile group of masked individuals" had gathered in front of the main union march. Tear gas was used to clear a square in the south-western city of Bordeaux and a teenager was hurt in a stabbing involving a student at a high-school blockade in the Seine-Saint-Denis suburb of northern Paris.

But the trouble was not on the scale seen during the last big day of protests, on Saturday 17 October, when more than 2,000 stores were looted and historic buildings set on fire.

The provisional numbers reported on Thursday were higher than on 17 October, considered the biggest day of rallies until now when the interior ministry said 550,000 people had turned out and the unions counted 800,000.

Earlier on Thursday the leader of the CFDT union, Marylise LÃ©on, said she would be happy if the protests matched that earlier level.

A car was set alight during a protest in the centre of Lyon

A coalition of unions across the public and private sector had urged workers, pensioners and young people to join Thursday's day of strikes to intensify pressure on the government. In particular they are seeking a rise in the minimum wage, or Smic, which is currently â¬12.31 (Â£10) an hour.

Sophie Binet told reporters at the start of a march in central Paris that protesters had "just reached a new level in their mobilisation" and were demanding immediate answers.

An estimated 9.89% of civil servants were taking part in the strikes, according to government figures.

Disruption was reported in schools, hospitals and public transport, and the Eiffel Tower and some exhibition rooms at the Louvre museum in Paris had to close.

Workers were also asked to halt production at oil refineries and France was expected to import more electricity than it produced on Thursday because of disruption at nuclear plants.

Disruption was already reported at schools on Thursday morning

The head of the CFDT complained that it was not just the minimum wage that needed to increase. "We are asking for a net increase of â¬100 a month," Marylise LÃ©on explained.

The government has sought to appease public anger over rising prices, bringing down the cost of fuel by releasing 10 million barrels of diesel from strategic reserves. Ministers have also promised school students to reform the unpopular Parcoursup university admissions platform and eventually replace it by 2028.

The prime minister said early on Thursday evening that Monday's "social conference" with unions and employers would seek to "provide answers to questions surrounding purchasing power and wages".

However, the government by its own admission has little room to manoeuvre, as it looks to make tens of billions of euros in savings, in a bid to cut the budget deficit to 5% of GDP.

The education ministry said that 16% of teachers had gone on strike on Thursday, and the unions said disruption had affected more than one in four lycÃ©es, which cater for 15-18 year-olds, as well as the vast majority of universities.

"Now it's not only about [investing in] public services in education and higher education, it's about all of the public services in France," the vice-president of the Unef student union, Alice De Brito, told the BBC.

Meanwhile, a revived gilets jaunes (yellow-vest) movement that staged a revolt against the high cost of living several years ago has called for further blockades and protests on Friday and Saturday too.

#30. One dead and another missing in suspected grizzly bear attack in Canada

The incident took place in the same province where a grizzly attacked a group of schoolchildren last November

A mother is dead and her daughter is missing in western Canada after a suspected grizzly bear attack in the same community where a bear attacked a group of schoolchildren earlier in the year.

On Tuesday, the British Columbia conservation officer service said in a social media post that it had launched a search for the two missing people on the weekend, and that during the search on Sunday an officer found the mother’s body. The search for her 19-year-old daughter is ongoing.

“Our thoughts are with the families of the victims during this tragic time,” Inspector Jeff Tyre said in a video posted online.

Jenny Balcarras, an art therapist, and her daughter were reported missing by relatives over the weekend. A family member posted on Facebook about them, later updating it after the conservation service announced the suspected attack to indicate that their vehicle had been found at a trailhead near Bella Coola, British Columbia.

The suspected attack happened at the Snooka West trail area, about 8km (4.97 miles) east of Bella Coola, on the province’s central coast.

Bella Coola, a community of roughly 2,100 people, sits at the end of a fjord along the province’s central coast. Its remote location and abundant food sources mean that grizzly bears are a common sight in the region, which is informally known as the Great Bear rainforest.

Grizzly attacks in the province are exceedingly rare, but last November, 11 people, some as young as nine years old, were injured when a grizzly emerged from the forest near 4 Mile, a Nuxalk community, and attacked a school group on a lunch break alongside a walking trail. Despite an extensive search, the bear was never located.

“While the full circumstances are still unfolding, we understand first-hand the trauma this incident will inflict upon the victims’ family members, the victims of last year’s attack and the community at large,” said Samuel Schooner, elected chief of the Nuxalk Nation, on Tuesday. “Our thoughts are with the victim’s family as they learn of this tragic event.”

Schooner said a trapping effort to find the bear was under way and asked media to refrain from contacting community members.

#28. Paraguay President Santiago Peña resigns after bank collapse

Paraguay's President Santiago PeÃ±a has announced his resignation following a political crisis sparked by the collapse of a bank linked to him.

"I want to apologise to the people if at any point I failed," he said in a video message on social media. "It was never my intention to betray the trust you placed in me."

The crisis erupted after Ueno Bank entered an insolvency process, which froze all operations. Lawmakers accused PeÃ±a, who was a shareholder until last year in the company that owned the bank, of conflicts of interest and misconduct.

PeÃ±a rejected suggestions of impropriety. His resignation came as Paraguay's lower house of congress voted in favour of pursuing an impeachment trial.

Photos taken near Paraguay's Congress building on Thursday show police officers clashing with protesters demanding PeÃ±a be removed from office.

Paraguay's congress must approve the resignation of a president. But a special session convened on Thursday to address his resignation was adjourned in favour of continuing with the impeachment process.

Congress has scheduled a special session for Friday, when PeÃ±a is expected to present his defence, according to Reuters news agency.

In a post on X, PeÃ±a confirmed he would appear for Senate impeachment proceedings, adding that he would do so to "exercise my right to a defence".

He wrote: "I will provide the information requested of me. Citizens deserve to know the truth, without cover-ups and without anyone being above the law."

PeÃ±a earlier said his resignation was to protect stability, social peace and democratic institutions.

Ueno Bank has said it has about three million customers, equivalent to about 60% of Paraguay's adult population.

It held pensions and some other public funds and, after its collapse earlier this week, millions could not access their accounts.

The central bank seized the digital lender, and people took to the streets protesting and demanding PeÃ±a's resignation.

A new president of the Central Bank of Paraguay has been appointed and Congress has approved a process to pay depositors.

Protesters gathered near the Paraguayan Congress on Thursday

PeÃ±a won election in 2023 representing Paraguay's ruling conservative Colorado Party, beating a centre-left challenger.

The Colorado party dropped impeachment proceedings against Vice President Pedro Alliana, who is next in line for the presidency after PeÃ±a.

#27. Mother and daughter killed in grizzly bear attack, Canadian authorities confirm

Conservation officers say more than one bear may have been involved in attack on women near their home

Search teams in Canada have discovered the body of a 20-year-old woman and confirmed that both she and her mother were killed in an attack that conservation officers now believe could have involved multiple grizzly bears.

Late on Tuesday, conservation officers in British Columbia said the two women, who were reported missing over the weekend, were located near a trail a short distance from their home.

The discovery has shaken a community where, earlier in the year, a grizzly attacked a group of schoolchildren and their teachers.

“Our thoughts are with the families of the victims during this tragic time,” Inspector Jeff Tyre of the British Columbia Conservation Officer Service said in a video posted online. Tyre later said the conservation service was investigating the possibility that more than one bear was involved in the fatal attack.

“We appreciate people’s concerns and the desire to assist but it is critical the public stay out of the area – both due to the danger and to allow officers to do their work,” he said.

Jenny Balcarras, an art therapist, and her daughter Selah Reed were reported missing by relatives over the weekend. A family member posted on Facebook about them, later updating it after the conservation service announced the suspected attack to indicate that their vehicle had been found at a trailhead near Bella Coola, British Columbia. Balcarras’s remains were found first and Reed was located a day later.

A fundraiser for Balcarras started by her family mourned a woman who had recently moved to Bella Coola, a community of roughly 2,100 people, for a new job and “to start a new chapter” of her life. She leaves behind three adult children and a grandchild.

“[Jenny] was working toward building a future and had not yet had the opportunity to establish savings or put aside funds to help her family with unexpected expenses such as these,” wrote relative Mary Melissa Smith. “Tragically, no one could have anticipated that her family would be faced with such an unimaginable loss.”

The family also mourned 20-year-old Selah, who had her “whole life ahead of her” before the attack.

“She was only beginning her journey into adulthood, making this loss even more heartbreaking,” wrote Smith.

Both Balcarras and Reed had lived in communities in remote and rugged parts of the province, as well as time in Alaska.

The suspected attack happened at the Snooka West trail area, about 8km (5 miles) east of Bella Coola, on the province’s central coast.

Bella Coola, which sits at the end of a fjord along the province’s central coast, is a remote location, and abundant food sources mean that grizzly bears are a common sight in the region, which is informally known as the Great Bear rainforest.

Grizzly attacks in the province are exceedingly rare, but last November, 11 people, some as young as nine years old, were injured when a grizzly emerged from the forest near 4 Mile, a Nuxalk community, and attacked a school group on a lunch break alongside a walking trail. Despite an extensive search, the bear was never located.

Samuel Schooner, elected chief of the Nuxalk Nation, said on Tuesday: “Our thoughts are with the victim’s family as they learn of this tragic event.” Schooner said a trapping effort to find the bear was under way and asked media to refrain from contacting community members.

The province’s premier-designate, Lorne Doerkson, called the news “heartbreaking” and said he was thinking of the victims’ family and the Nuxalk Nation.

“At a time of such profound grief and uncertainty, my thoughts are with everyone who is hurting and with all those supporting one another through an incredibly difficult day.”

#22. California governor commutes sentences for state's 548 death row inmates

California Governor Gavin Newsom's term in office comes to an end in January

California Governor Gavin Newsom has commuted the death sentences of all 548 people on death row in the state, changing them instead to life in prison.

"We can now let these offenders die in obscurity while we forever remember and honour the victims," he said in a letter.

The decision to spare death row inmates is largely symbolic in a state that has not executed an inmate since 2006, though experts say it could save millions of dollars in court costs each year. Only 13 inmates have been executed since voters reinstated the death penalty in 1978.

The decision comes as Newsom, the Democratic leader of the most populous US state, has been discussed as a possible 2028 contender for president.

Newsom, who is term limited, is winding down his final months in office. Fellow Democrat Xavier Becerra is projected to take over after the midterm elections on Tuesday.

Newsom explained in his letter that he is exercising his constitutional clemency authority to commute the sentences to life without the possibility of parole.

"After nearly 30 years in public office, I have come to understand the limits of what government can justly accomplish," Newsom said. "I take this action because I firmly believe that state-sanctioned killing is incompatible with California's values, undermines the foundations of our legal system and diminishes each of us".

California has historically had the largest population of death row inmates. Newsom had previously ordered a halt to the death penalty in the state in 2019.

Americans' views on death penalty after failed execution in Tennessee

Describing his decision as a difficult but "righteous action", Newsom said that although many Californians feel that those who commit brutal crimes deserve to die, he has concluded that "the state cannot morally kill them".

He said that by every measure, California's system for administering the death penalty "is a failure", and that it has not and cannot be fairly applied, resulting in the conviction of innocent people.

Life without the possibility of parole is the most severe punishment he can lawfully impose and that the state can ethically enforce, he said.

He acknowledged that his clemency action will anger and hurt some Californians and said that it will "always weigh heavily on me".

"As governor, I alone bear responsibility for this decision and its impact, just as I alone would bear responsibility for each execution," he wrote.

"For this reason, I am compelled to follow my conscience and, as authorised by our Constitution, take this action for the welfare of the State of California."

Because individual states have the power to prosecute crimes and order executions, much of the power lies with state governors.

Bernard E Harcourt, a law and civil liberties professor at Columbia University, told the BBC that it is not unprecedented for a potential presidential candidate to come out as an abolitionist in the run up to elections - despite the overall popularity of the death penalty in the US.

He noted that FranÃ§ois Mitterrand did precisely that in his presidential campaign in France in 1981, taking "the higher ethical ground and was rewarded by the public for that".

"I detect a very similar ethical positioning in Governor Newsom's statements about the commutations. And here too, it may well be something that the voters respect," he said.

San Quentin State Prison's used to house California's death row adjustment centre

Newsom's decision comes after the high-profile botched execution of a woman in Tennessee in late September and while executions have been on the rise in the US. There are three executions scheduled for Thursday in Indiana, Florida and Alabama.

At least four of seven judges on the California Supreme Court will have to sign off on the commutations, CalMatters reported.

It is unclear how Newsom's decision will affect condemned inmates like Keith Doolin, 53, who was sentenced to death in 1996 for murdering two sex workers, Inez Espinoza and Peggy Tucker, and shooting four others. He maintains his innocence.

Doolin lived on death row at San Quentin for decades, until he was moved to another prison in 2025 when the governor announced the state would reform the prison for all but those on death row.

Doolin's mother, Donna Larsen, told the BBC that the news of Newsom's commutations - which includes Doolin - brought mixed emotions.

"At least the execution chamber will not be reconstructed, and no more killing by the people of the state of California," she said.

Anthony D Romero, the executive director of the American Civil Liberties Union, applauded Newsom's action.

"These commutations reflect what justice demands: an end to a racist, error-prone, and inhumane punishment that has burdened California for far too long," Romero said.

"By taking this step, Gov. Newsom has affirmed that the death penalty has no place in California and he showed real courage by confronting racialised injustice head-on."

Nathan Hochman, the top prosecutor in Los Angeles County, said that while Newsom has the power to commute sentences, only state legislature or voters can eliminate the death penalty.

"Californians have been asked on two separate ballot initiatives to abolish the death penalty and both times they have chosen to keep it for the most serious crimes," Hochman said in a statement.

How widely used is the death penalty in the US?

On the federal level, President Donald Trump, a long-time supporter of the death penalty, has described capital punishment as "an essential tool for deterring and punishing those who would commit the most heinous crimes and acts of lethal violence against American citizens".

In 2020, during his first term, he ended a nearly two-decade moratorium on the federal death penalty, by ordering 13 executions over a six-month period.

While the moratorium returned under President Biden, on the first day of Trump's second term in 2025, he signed an executive order that re-instated the death penalty.

No federal executions have been carried out since he signed it, however. In the US, both the federal government and local states authorities can order executions.

The death penalty is legal in 27 states, but has been abolished in 23 states and Washington DC, according to the Death Penalty Information Center, a national non-profit.

California, which primarily uses lethal injection for executions, used to house male death row prisoners in San Quentin State Prison north of San Francisco, and female inmates in the Central California Women's Facility in the Central Valley.

But a 2016 ballot measure allowed for their transfer to other facilities that provide a necessary level of security, according to the California Department of Corrections and Rehabilitation.

Why the death penalty is on the rise in US

#21. 'Leaders all over the world understand that Trump is only there for two more years & then he's gone'

In American politics, the possession of presidential power does not necessarily guarantee the capacity to exercise it. Few contemporary leaders have embodied this contradiction as dramatically as Donald Trump. Following what political scientist Ian Bremmer describes as the most consequential electoral reversal for a governing party in more than half a century, the founder of Eurasia Group and GZERO Media argues that the United States has entered a new political phase: the beginning of the end of Trump's capacity to dominate the domestic and international agenda. In this wide-ranging interview with France 24, Bremmer revisits his provocative thesis that America has already reached "peak Trump." Far from suggesting that the president has become powerless, he contends that his authority is increasingly constrained by congressional opposition, Republican discontent, unpopular foreign policy decisions and the growing awareness among international leaders that the present administration has a limited political horizon. The historical reversal is, in Bremmer's account, extraordinary: "Trump back in 2024 engineered for himself the biggest political comeback in American political history. And now, only two years later, the biggest loss in 50 years." At the heart of his analysis lies a fundamental distinction between institutional authority and political influence. Trump remains commander-in-chief, retains the instruments of executive government and continues to command extraordinary media attention. Yet Bremmer argues that the president's ability to translate those advantages into effective political outcomes is diminishing. The consequences, he suggests, extend far beyond Washington. From the war in Iran and the unresolved conflict in Ukraine to the future of transatlantic relations, America's allies and adversaries are beginning to adjust their strategic calculations to a post-Trump political landscape. As Bremmer puts it: "Those foreign leaders will start to engage more with the America that they expect to deal with after Trump is gone." Equally significant is his diagnosis of the domestic forces driving this transformation. Economic disillusionment, unfulfilled electoral promises and accusations of political self-interest have, in his assessment, undermined the very coalition that propelled Trump back to the White House. Meanwhile, the president's apparent preoccupation with monumental projects and personal legacy raises a deeper question about the relationship between political spectacle and the responsibilities of governance. Bremmer's conclusion is unambiguous: "Yes, two more years to go. But nowhere near the level of disruption that he had on the global stage at the beginning of this term." The interview ultimately raises a question of profound significance for American democracy and the international order: Can a president retain the formal powers of the world's most consequential political office while progressively losing the political authority necessary to shape its future? Bremmer believes that process is already underway.
